\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n}\bigg\{\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{T}+\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{T}

\displaystyle\ +\mathcal{H}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\mathcal{H}_{2}\bigg\}.

(37)

Therefore, for any vector
\theta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we have

\displaystyle\theta_{1}^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})\theta_{2}=\theta_{1}^{T}\mathcal{W}_{1}^{T}\Big(\frac{\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}}{n}-I_{r}\Big)\mathcal{W}_{2}\theta_{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\theta_{1}^{T}\Big(\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{T}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\mathcal{H}_{2}\Big)\theta_{2}.

(38)

By Bai-Yin law on eigenvalues of Wishart matrices
[
67
]
, there exists abolute constant
C>0
so that for any
t>1
,

P\bigg(\norm{\frac{\mZ^T\mZ}{n}-I_r}_{op}<t\sqrt{r/n}\bigg)\geq 1-2\exp(-Ct^2r),

which, combined with (
36
), implies

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})}\mathbb{P}\Big(

\displaystyle\Big|\theta_{1}^{T}\mathcal{W}_{1}^{T}({\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}}/{n}-I_{r})\mathcal{W}_{2}\theta_{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ t\mathcal{B}^{2}\|\theta_{1}\|_{2}\|\theta_{2}\|_{2}\sqrt{r/n}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\geq

\displaystyle\ 1-2\exp(-Ct^2r).

Now we will state a lemma which will be required to control the other terms on the right hand side of (
C-2
).

Lemma 3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
are independent Gaussian data matrices. Further suppose
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
are either deterministic or independent of both
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
so that for any
t>1
,

P\Big(\absolutevalue{x^T\mZ_1^T\mZ_2y}>t\|x\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}\sqrt{n}\Big)\leq\exp(-Cn)-\exp(t^2/2).

The proof of Lemma
3
follows directly setting
b=1
in the following Lemma, which is proved in Appendix
H-D
.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
are independent standard Gaussian data matrices, and
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k_{1}}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k_{2}}
are deterministic matrices with rank
a
and
b
, respectively. Let
a\leq b\leq n
.
Then there exists an absolute constant
C>0
so that for any
t\geq 0
, the following holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cn)-\exp(- t^2/2)
:

\|D^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}B\|_{op}\leq C\|D\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}\sqrt{n}\max\{\sqrt{b},t\}.

Lemma
3
, in conjunction with (
36
), implies that there exists an absolute constant
C>0
so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\Big|\theta_{1}^{T}\Big(\mathcal{W}_{1}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{T}+\mathcal{H}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\mathcal{H}_{2}\Big)\theta_{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ t\mathcal{B}^{2}\|\theta_{1}\|_{2}\|\theta_{2}\|_{2}n^{-1/2}

with
\mathbb{P}
probability at least
1-\exp(-Cn)-\exp(t^2/2)
for all
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
. Therefore, there exists
C>0
so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(|\theta_{1}^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}-{\Sigma}_{xy})\theta_{2}|\leq t\sqrt{r}\mathcal{B}^{2}\|\theta_{1}\|_{2}\|\theta_{2}\|_{2}n^{-1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ 1-\exp(-Cn)-\exp(-Ct^2).

(39)

for all
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
. Note that

\displaystyle T_{1}(i,k)\leq

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{\Big|\Big((\widehat{\Omega}_{n})_{k*}-({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})_{k*}\Big)^{T}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,yx}-{\Sigma}_{yx})\widehat{u}_{n,i}\Big|}_{T_{11}(i,k)}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\Big|\Big((\widehat{\Omega}_{n})_{k*}-({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})_{k*}\Big)^{T}{\Sigma}_{yx}\widehat{u}_{n,i}\Big|}_{T_{12}(i,k)}.

Now suppose
\theta_{1}=(\widehat{\Omega}_{n})_{k*}-({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})_{k*}
and
\theta_{2}=\widehat{u}_{n,i}
. By our assumption,

\|\theta_{1}\|_{2}\leq C_{\text{pre}}\sqrt{s({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})(\log q)/n}
with
\mathbb{P}
probability
1-o(1)
uniformly across
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
. We also showed that
\|\widehat{u}_{n,i}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{\mathcal{B}}
.
It is not had to see that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in[q],k\in[r]}T_{12}(i,k)\leq 2\mathcal{B}^{3/2}C_{\text{pre}}\sqrt{s({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})(\log q)/n}

(40)

with
\mathbb{P}
probability
1-o(1)
uniformly across
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
.
For
T_{11}
, observe that (
C-2
) applies because
\theta_{i}=(\widehat{\Omega}_{n})_{k*}-({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})_{k*}
and
\theta_{2}=\widehat{u}_{n,i}
are independent of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}
.
Thus we can write that for any
t>1
, there exists
C_{\mathcal{B}}>1
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})}\mathbb{P}\Big(|T_{11}(i,k)|>tC_{\mathcal{B}}C_{\text{pre}}\sqrt{rs({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})\log q}/n\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \exp(-Cn)+\exp(-Ct^2).

Applying union bound, we obtain that for any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\in[q]}\max_{k\in[r]}|T_{11}(i,k)|>\frac{tC_{\mathcal{B}}C_{\text{pre}}\sqrt{rs({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})\log q}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-Cn+\log(qr))+\exp(-Ct^2+\log(qr)).

Since
r<q
and
\log q=o(n)
, setting
t=2\sqrt{\log q}/C
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{P}\in\\
\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\in[q]}\max_{k\in[r]}|T_{11}(i,k)|>C_{\mathcal{B}}C_{\text{pre}}\sqrt{rs({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})}\frac{\log q}{n}\right)

is
o(1)
.
Using (
C-1
) and (
40
), one can show that

\epsilon_{n}=C_{\text{pre}}\sqrt{s({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})(\log(p+q))/n}\max\{\sqrt{r(\log q)/n},1\}

in this case.

C-
3
Case (C)

Note that when
\widehat{\Omega}_{n}={\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
,
T_{1}(i,k)=0
. Therefore, (
C-1
) implies
\epsilon_{n}=\sqrt{{s({\Sigma}_{y}^{-1})\log(p+q)}/{n}}
in this case.

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
2

Since the proof for
U
and
V
follows in a similar way, we will only consider the support recovery of
U
. The proof for both cases follows a common structure. Therefore, we will elaborate the common structure first.
Since the model
\mathcal{P}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
is fairly large, we will work with a smaller submodel. Specifically, we will consider a subclass of the single spike models, i.e.,
r=1
. Because we are concerned with only the support recovery of the left singular vectors, we fix
\beta_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
so that
\|\beta_{0}\|_{2}=1
. We also fix
\rho\in(0,1)
and consider the subset

\mathcal{E}\subset\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|\alpha\|_{2}=1\}
. Both
\rho
and
\mathcal{E}
will be chosen later. We restrict our attention to the submodel
\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})
given by

\displaystyle\bigg\{

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(1,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})\ :\ \mathbb{P}\equiv N_{p+q}(0,\Sigma)\text{ where }\Sigma

\displaystyle\ \text{ is of the form }\eqref{def: sigma in Theorem 3}\text{ with }\alpha\in\mathcal{E},\beta=\beta_{0}\bigg\},

where (
41
) is as follows:

\Sigma=\begin{bmatrix}I_{p}&\rho\alpha\beta^{T}\\
\rho\beta\alpha^{T}&I_{q}\end{bmatrix}.

(41)

That
\Sigma
is positive definite for
\rho\in(0,1)
can be shown either using elementary linear algebra or the the hidden variable representation (
35
).
During the proof of part (B), we will choose
\mathcal{E}
so that
\texttt{Sig}_{x}^{2}\leq(B^{2}-1)(\log(p-s_x))/8n
, which will ensure that
\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})\subset\mathcal{P}_{\texttt{Sig}}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
as well.

Note that for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})
,
U
corresponds to
\alpha
, and hence
D(U)=D(\alpha)
.
Therefore for the proof of both parts, it suffices to show that for any decoder
\widehat{D}_{\alpha}
of
D(\alpha)
,

\inf_{\widehat{D}_{\alpha}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{E})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\widehat{D}_{\alpha}\neq D(\alpha)\Big)>1/2.

(42)

In both of the proofs, our
\mathcal{E}
will be a finite set. Our goal is to choose
\mathcal{E}
so that
\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})
is structurally rich enough to guarantee (
42
), yet lends itself to easy computations.
The guidance for choosing
\mathcal{E}
comes from our main technical tool for this proof, which is Fano’s inequality. We use the verson of Fano’s inequality in
[
53
]
(Fano’s Lemma). Applied to our problem, this inequality yields

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{D}_{\alpha}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\widehat{D}_{\alpha}\neq D(\alpha)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ 1-\dfrac{\frac{\sum_{\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})}KL(\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n}|\mathbb{P}_{2}^{n})}{|\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})|^{2}}+\log 2}{\log(|\mM(s_x,s_y,\rho,\mE)|-1)},

(43)

where
\mathbb{P}_{n}
denotes the product measure corresponding to
n
i.i.d. observations from
\mathbb{P}
. We also have the following result for product measures,

KL(\mathbb{P}_{1}^{n}|\mathbb{P}_{2}^{n})=nKL(\mathbb{P}_{1}|\mathbb{P}_{2})
. Moreover, when

\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})
with left singular vectors
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, respectively,

KL(\mathbb{P}_{1}|\mathbb{P}_{2})=\log\frac{\text{det}(\Sigma_{2})}{\text{det}(\Sigma_{1})}-(p+q)+Tr(\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\Sigma_{1}),

where
\text{det}(\Sigma_{1})=\text{det}(\Sigma_{2})=1-\rho^{2}
by Lemma
13
, and

-(p+q)+Tr(\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\Sigma_{1})=\frac{2\rho^{2}}{1-\rho^{2}}\bigg(1-(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})\|\beta_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)

by Lemma
14
.
Noting
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
, and
\beta_{0}
are unit vectors, we derive

KL(\mathbb{P}_{1}|\mathbb{P}_{2})=\rho^{2}(\|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\|^{2}_{2})/(1-\rho^{2})
.
Therefore, in our case, (
D
) reduces to

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{D}_{\alpha}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\widehat{D}_{\alpha}\neq D(\alpha)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ 1-\dfrac{n\rho^{2}\sup_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathcal{E}}\|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\|^{2}/(1-\rho^{2})+\log 2}{\log(|\mE|-1)}.

(44)

Thus, to ensure the right hand side of (
D
) is non-negligible, the key is to choose
\mathcal{E}
so that the
\alpha
’s in
\mathcal{E}
are close in
l_{2}
norm, but
|\mathcal{E}|
is sufficiently large. Note that the above ensures that distinguishing the
\alpha
’s in
\mathcal{E}
is difficult.

D-A

Proof of part (A)

Note that our main job is to choose
\mathcal{E}
and
\rho
suitably.
Let us denote

\alpha_{0}=(\underbrace{1/\sqrt{s}_{x},\ldots,1/\sqrt{s}_{x}}_{s_{x}\text{ many }},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{p-s_{x}\text{ many }}).

We generate a class of
\alpha
’s by replacing one of the
1/\sqrt{s}_{x}
’s in
\alpha_{0}
by
0
, and one of the zero’s in
\alpha_{0}
by
1/\sqrt{s}_{x}
. A typical
\alpha
obtained this way looks like

\alpha=\Big(\underbrace{1/\sqrt{s}_{x},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{0}},\ldots 1/\sqrt{s}_{x}}_{s_{x}\text{ many }},\underbrace{0,\ldots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{1/\sqrt{s_{x}}}},\ldots,0}_{p-s_{x}\text{ many }}\Big).

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the class, which consists of
\alpha_{0}
, and all such resulting
\alpha
’s. Note that
|\mathcal{E}|=s_{x}(p-s_{x})
, and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
satisfy

\|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{0}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{0}\|^{2}_{2}\leq 4s_{x}^{-1}.

Because
p>s_{x}>1
, we have

\log(s_x(p-s_x)-1)\geq\log(p-s_x).

Therefore, (
D
) leads to

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{D}_{\alpha}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})}\mathbb{P}\Big(\widehat{D}_{\alpha}\neq D(\alpha)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ 1-\frac{4\rho^{2}ns_{x}^{-1}/(1-\rho^{2})+\log 2}{\log(p-s_x)},

which is bounded below by
1/2
whenever

s_{x}>\frac{8\rho^{2}n}{(1-\rho^{2})\{\log(p-s_x)-\log 4\}},

which follows if

s_{x}>\frac{16\rho^{2}n}{(1-\rho^{2})\log(p-s_x)}

because
4=\sqrt{16}<\sqrt{p-s_{x}}
. To get the best bound on
s_{x}
, we choose the value of
\rho
which minimizes
\rho^{2}/(1-\rho^{2})
for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
, that is
\rho=1/\mathcal{B}
.
Plugging in
\rho=1/\mathcal{B}
, the proof follows.

D-B

Proof of part (B)

Suppose each
\alpha\in\mathcal{E}
is of the following form

\alpha=\Big(\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{s_{x}-1\text{ many }},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}z},0,\ldots,0}_{p-s_{x}+1\text{ many }}\Big).

We fix
z\in(0,1)
, and hence
b=\sqrt{(1-z^{2})/(s_{x}-1)}
is also fixed. We will choose the value of
\rho
and
z
later so that
\mathcal{P}_{\texttt{Sig}}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})\supset\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})
.
Since
z
is fixed, such an
\alpha
can be chosen in
p-s_{x}+1
ways. Therefore
|\mathcal{E}|=p-s_{x}+1
. Also note that for
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
,

\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2z^{2}
.
Therefore (
D
) implies

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{D}_{\alpha}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\rho,\mathcal{E})}P\Big(\widehat{D}_{\alpha}\neq D(\alpha)\Big)

(45)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ 1-\dfrac{2n\rho^{2}z^{2}/(1-\rho^{2})+\log 2}{\log(p-s_x)},

(46)

which is greater than
1/2
whenever

z^{2}<\frac{1-\rho^{2}}{4n\rho^{2}}\log(\frac{p-s_x}{4}),

which holds if

z^{2}=\frac{1-\rho^{2}}{8n\rho^{2}}\log(p-s_x)

because
16<p-s_{x}
.
To get the best bound on
z
, we choose the value of
\rho
for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
which maximizes
(1-\rho^{2})/\rho^{2}
, that is
\rho=1/\mathcal{B}
. Thus (
42
) is satisfied when
\rho=1/\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{E}
corresponds to

z^{2}=(\mathcal{B}^{2}-1)\log(p-s_x)/(8n).

Since the minimal signal strength
\texttt{Sig}_{x}
for any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B}^{-1},\mathcal{E})
equals
\min(z,b)\leq z
, we have
\mathcal{P}_{\texttt{Sig}}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})\supset\mathcal{M}(s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B}^{-1},\mathcal{E})
, which
completes the proof.

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
3

We first introduce some notations and terminologies that are required for the proof.
For
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we denote
w!=\prod_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}!
and
x^{w}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{w_{i}}
. In low-degree polynomial literature, when
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, the notation
|w|
is commonly used to denote the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}
for sake of simplicity. We also follow the above convention.
Here the notation
|\cdot|
should not be confused with the absolute value of real numbers.
Also, for any function
f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, and
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
, we denote

\partial_{t}^{w}f(t)=\frac{\partial^{|w|}}{\partial t_{1}^{w_{1}}\cdots\partial t_{r}^{w_{r}}}f(t).

We will also use the shorthand notation
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}
to denote
\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\pi_{x},\beta\sim\pi_{y}}
sometimes.

Our analysis relies on the Hermite polynomial, which we will discuss here very briefly. For a detailed account on the Hermite polynomials, see Chapter V of
[
62
]
. The univariate Hermite polynomials of degree
k
will be denoted by
h_{k}
. For
k\geq 0
, the univariate Hermite polynomials
h_{k}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
are defined recursively as follows:

\displaystyle h_{0}(x)=1,\quad h_{1}(x)=xh_{0}(x),\quad\ldots,

\displaystyle\ h_{k+1}(x)=xh_{k}(x)-h_{k}^{\prime}(x).

The normalized univariate Hermite polynomials are given by
\widehat{h}_{k}(x)=h_{k}(x)/\sqrt{k!}
. The univariate Hermite polynomials form an orthogonal basis of
L_{2}(N(0,1))
.
For
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, the
m
-variate Hermite polynomials are given by
H_{w}(y)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}h_{w_{i}}(y_{i})
, where
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The normalized version
\widehat{H}_{w}
of
H_{w}
equals

H_{w}/\sqrt{w!}
. The polynomials
\widehat{H}_{w}
’s form an orthogonal basis of
L_{2}(N_{m}(0,I_{m}))
. We denote by
\Pi_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}
the linear span of all
n(p+q)
-variate Hermite polynomials of degree at most
D_{n}
.
Since
\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}
is the projection of
\mathbb{L}_{n}
on
\Pi^{\leq D_{n}}
, it then follows that

\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathbb{Z}^{n(p+q)}\\
|w|\leq D_{n}\end{subarray}}\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},\widehat{H}_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}^{2}.

(47)

From now on, the degree-index vector
w
of
\widehat{H}_{w}
or
H_{w}
will be assumed to lie in
\mathbb{Z}^{n(p+q)}
. We will partition
w
into
n
components, which gives
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
, where
w_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p+q}
for each
i\in[n]
. Clearly,
i
here corresponds to the
i
-th observation. We also separate each
w_{i}
into two parts
w_{i}^{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}
and
w_{i}^{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q}
so that
w_{i}=(w_{i}^{x},w_{i}^{y})
. We will also denote
w^{x}=(w^{x}_{1},\ldots,w^{x}_{n})
, and
w_{y}=(w^{y}_{1},\ldots,w^{y}_{n})
. Note that
w^{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{np}
and
w^{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{nq}
, but
w\neq(w^{x},w^{y})
in general, although
|w|=|w^{x}|+|w^{y}|
.

Now we state the main lemmas which yields the value of
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
. The first lemma, proved in Appendix
H-C
, gives the form of the inner products
\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},\widehat{H}_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
.

Lemma 5
.

Suppose
w
is as defined above and
\mathbb{L}_{n}
is as in (
20
). Then it holds that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},\widehat{H}_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \begin{cases}\frac{\mathcal{B}^{-|w|}}{w!}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[1\{\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}\alpha^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{x}_{i}}\beta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{y}_{i}}\bigg]\bigg\}^{2}\\
\times\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}{|w^{x}_{i}|!}\bigg)^{2}\quad\text{ if }\quad|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|\text{ for all }i\in[n],\\
0\quad\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}

Here the priors
\pi_{x}
and
\pi_{y}
are the Rademacher priors defined in (
17
).

Our next lemma uses Lemma
5
to give the form of
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
. This lemma uses replicas of
\alpha
and
\beta
. Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\sim\pi_{x}
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\sim\pi_{y}
are all independent Rademacher priors, where
\pi_{x}
and
\pi_{y}
are defined as in (
17
). We overload notation, and use
\mathbb{E}_{\pi}
to denote the expectation under
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,
\beta_{1}
, and
\beta_{2}
.

Lemma 6
.

Suppose
W
is the indicator function of the event

\{\|\alpha_{1}\|_{2}\|\beta_{1}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B},\ \|\alpha_{2}\|_{2}\|\beta_{2}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}
. Then
For any
D_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
equals

\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W\sum_{d=0}^{\left\lfloor D_{n}/2\right\rfloor}{d+n-1\choose d}\bigg\{\mathcal{B}^{-2}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})\bigg\}^{d}\bigg].

The proof of Lemma
6
is also deferred to
Appendix
H-C
.
We remark in passing that the negative binomial series expansion yields

(1-x)^{-n}=\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}{n+d-1\choose d}x^{d},\quad\text{for }|x|<1,

(48)

whose
D_{n}
-th order truncation equals

\bigg((1-x)^{-n}\bigg)^{\leq D_{n}}=\sum_{d=0}^{D_{n}}{n+d-1\choose d}x^{d}.

Note that
W
is nonzero if and only if
\|\alpha_{1}\|_{2}\|\beta_{1}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}
and
\|\alpha_{2}\|_{2}\|\beta_{2}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}
, which, by Cauchy Schwarz inequality, implies

|(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})|<\mathcal{B}^{2}.

Thus
|\mathcal{B}^{-2}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})|<1
when
W=1
. Hence Lemma
6
can also be written as

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}\left[W\bigg\{\bigg(1-\mathcal{B}^{-2}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})\bigg)^{-n}\bigg\}^{\leq\left\lfloor D_{n}/2\right\rfloor}\right].

Now we are ready to prove Theorem
3
.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Our first task is to get rid of
W
from the expression of
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
in Lemma
6
.
However, we can not directly bound
W
by one since the term
(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{d}W
may be negative for odd
d\in\mathbb{N}
.
We claim that

\mathbb{E}[(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{d}W]=0
if
d\in\mathbb{N}
is odd.
To see this, first we write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{d}W\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}W\Big|\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\Big](\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{d}\Big].

(49)

Note that

(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}W\mid\beta_{1},\beta_{2}

has the same distribution as

\displaystyle 1\{\|\alpha_{1}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\|\beta_{1}\|_{2}^{-1}\}1\{\|\alpha_{2}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\|\beta_{2}\|_{2}^{-1}\}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2}).

Notice from (
17
) that marginally,
\alpha_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-\alpha_{1}
, and
\alpha_{1}
is independent of
\alpha_{2}
,
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
. Therefore,

(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})W\mid\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})W\mid\beta_{1},\beta_{2}.

Hence, conditional on
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
,
(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})W
is a symmetric random variable, and
\mathbb{E}[(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}W^{d}\mid\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]=0
for any odd positive integer
d
. Since
W
is binary random variable,
W^{d}=W
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}[(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}W\mid\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]=0
as well for an odd number
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus the claim follows from (
49
).
Therefore, from Lemma
6
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W\sum_{d=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{\left\lfloor\frac{D_{n}}{2}\right\rfloor}{2}\right\rfloor}{2d+n-1\choose 2d}\bigg\{\mathcal{B}^{-2}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})\bigg\}^{2d}\bigg].

Observe that
\left\lfloor\left\lfloor D_{n}/2\right\rfloor/2\right\rfloor\leq D_{n}/4
. Hence,
\left\lfloor\left\lfloor D_{n}/2\right\rfloor/2\right\rfloor\leq\left\lfloor D_{n}/4\right\rfloor
. Also the summands in the last expression are non-negative. Therefore, using the fact that
W\leq 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[\sum_{d=0}^{\left\lfloor D_{n}/4\right\rfloor}{2d+n-1\choose 2d}\bigg\{\mathcal{B}^{-2}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})\bigg\}^{2d}\bigg].

(50)

Our next step is to simplify the above bound on
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
.
To that end, define the random variables

\xi_{i}=\alpha_{1i}\alpha_{2i}
for
i\in[p]
, and
\xi^{\prime}_{j}=\beta_{1j}\beta_{2j}
for
j\in[q]
.
Denoting

\nu=({s_{x}}/{p})^{2},\quad\text{ and }\quad\omega=(s_{y}/q)^{2},

we note that

\displaystyle\xi_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{+1}{s_{x}}&w.p.\ \nu/2\\
\frac{-1}{s_{x}}&w.p.\ \nu/2\\
0&w.p.\ 1-\nu,\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}\frac{+1}{s_{y}}&w.p.\ \omega/2\\
\frac{-1}{s_{y}}&w.p.\ \omega/2\\
0&w.p.\ 1-\omega.\end{cases}

Also, since
\xi_{i}
and
\xi_{j}
’s are symmetric,
\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}^{2k+1}
and
\mathbb{E}\xi_{j}^{2k+1}
vanishes for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then for any
d\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{2d}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{2d}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ E_{\pi_{x}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\xi_{i}\bigg)^{2d}\bigg]E_{\pi_{y}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{q}\xi^{\prime}_{j}\bigg)^{2d}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
|z|=2d\end{subarray}}\frac{(2d)!}{z!}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\xi_{i}^{z_{i}}\Big]\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|l|=2d\end{subarray}}\frac{(2d)!}{l!}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\Big[(\xi^{\prime}_{j})^{l_{j}}\Big]\bigg)

by Fact
6
. Since the odd moments of
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
vanish,
the above equals

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
|z|=d\end{subarray}}\frac{(2d)!}{(2z)!}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\xi_{i}^{2z_{i}}\Big]\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|l|=d\end{subarray}}\frac{(2d)!}{(2l)!}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\mathbb{E}\Big[(\xi^{\prime}_{j})^{2l_{j}}\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
|z|=d\end{subarray}}\frac{\nu^{|D(z)|}(2d)!}{(2z)!}\prod_{i=1}^{p}s_{x}^{-2z_{i}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \times\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|l|=d\end{subarray}}\frac{\omega^{|D(z)|}(2d)!}{(2l)!}\prod_{j=1}^{q}s_{y}^{-2l_{j}}\bigg),

where we remind the readers that
|D(z)|
denotes the cardinality of the support of
z
for any vector
z
. The above implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{2d}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{2d}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (s_{x}s_{y})^{-2d}\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
|z|=d\end{subarray}}\frac{(2d)!}{(2z)!}\nu^{|D(z)|}}_{\mathcal{J}(d;p)}\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|l|=d\end{subarray}}\frac{(2d)!}{(2l)!}\nu^{|D(l)|}}_{\mathcal{J}(d;q)}.

Plugging the above into (
E
) yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sum_{d=0}^{\left\lfloor D_{n}/4\right\rfloor}{2d+n-1\choose 2d}(s_{x}s_{y})^{-2d}\mathcal{B}^{-4d}\mathcal{J}(d;p)\mathcal{J}_{d}(d;q)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\ \sum_{d=0}^{\left\lfloor D_{n}/4\right\rfloor}\bigg(\frac{(2d+n-1)e}{2d}\bigg)^{2d}(s_{x}s_{y})^{-2d}\mathcal{B}^{-4d}\mathcal{J}(d;p)\mathcal{J}(d;q),

where (a) follows since
{a\choose b}\leq(ae/b)^{b}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let us denote
\mu_{x}=\sqrt{ne}/(\sqrt{p}\mathcal{B})
and
\mu_{y}=\sqrt{ne}/(\sqrt{q}\mathcal{B})
.
By (
21
),
\mu_{x},\mu_{y}<1/\sqrt{3}

and

D_{n}\leq\frac{\min\{s_{y}^{2},s_{x}^{2}\}\mathcal{B}^{2}}{ne}.

Therefore we have

\mu_{x}^{2}D_{n}<\frac{s_{x}^{2}}{p}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu_{y}^{2}D_{n}<\frac{s_{y}^{2}}{q}.

Hence Lemma 4.5 of
[
36
]
implies that for any
11\leq d\leq D_{n}
,

\mathcal{J}(d;p)\lesssim(2d)!{p\choose d}\sqrt{d}e^{d^{2}/p+d/2}2^{-3d/2}\mu_{x}^{-2d}\nu^{d},

\mathcal{J}(d;q)\lesssim(2d)!{q\choose d}\sqrt{d}e^{d^{2}/q+d/2}2^{-3d/2}\mu_{y}^{-2d}\omega^{d}.

For
d\geq 1
, Theorem 5 of
[
68
]
gives

(2d)!\leq\frac{(2d)^{2d+1}e^{-2d}\sqrt{2\pi}}{\sqrt{2d-1}}.

Also since
{p\choose d}\leq(pe/d)^{d}
, we have

\mathcal{J}(d;p)\lesssim(2d)^{2d+1/2}\bigg(\frac{pe}{d}\bigg)^{d}\sqrt{d}e^{d^{2}/p-d}2^{-3d/2}\mu_{x}^{-2d}\nu^{d},

\mathcal{J}(d;q)\lesssim(2d)^{2d+1/2}\bigg(\frac{qe}{d}\bigg)^{d}\sqrt{d}e^{d^{2}/q-d}2^{-3d/2}\mu_{y}^{-2d}\omega^{d},

leading to

\mathcal{J}(d;p)\mathcal{J}(d;q)\lesssim d^{2d+2}e^{d^{2}/p+d^{2}/q}2^{d+1}(\mu_{x}\mu_{y})^{-2d}(\nu p)^{d}(\omega q)^{d}.

Therefore
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
is bounded by a constant multiple of

\displaystyle\sum_{d=11}^{\left\lfloor D_{n}/4\right\rfloor}\bigg(\frac{(2d+n-1)e}{2d}\bigg)^{2d}(s_{x}s_{y})^{-2d}d^{2d+2}e^{d^{2}/p+d^{2}/q}

\displaystyle\ \times 2^{d+1}(\mu_{x}\mu_{y})^{-2d}(\nu p)^{d}(\omega q)^{d}\mathcal{B}^{-4d}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\ \sum_{d=11}^{\left\lfloor D_{n}/4\right\rfloor}d\bigg\{\frac{\mathcal{B}^{-4}(2d+n-1)^{2}e^{2}}{2\mu_{x}^{2}\mu_{y}^{2}pq}\bigg\}^{d}e^{d^{2}/p+d^{2}/q}.

Since
D_{n}^{2}\leq\min\{p,q\}
, it follows that
e^{d^{2}/p+d^{2}/q}\leq e^{2}
. Note that the above sum converges if

(D_{n}/2+n-1)^{2}e^{2}<2\mathcal{B}^{4}\mu_{x}^{2}\mu_{y}^{2}pq=2n^{2}e^{2},

or equivalently
(D_{n}/2+n-1)^{2}<2n^{2}
, which is satisfied for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
since
D_{n}<n
. Thus the proof follows.
∎

Appendix F
Proof of Theorem
4

We invoke the decomposition of
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}
in (
C-2
). But first, we will derive a simplified form for the matrices
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
in (
C-2
). Note that we can write
\mathcal{H}_{1}
as

\mathcal{H}_{1}={\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}(I_{p}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U\Lambda U^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}){\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}.

Let us denote

B_{x}=\text{diag}(\underbrace{1-\Lambda_{1},\ldots,1-\Lambda_{r}}_{r\ \text{times}},\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{p-r\ \text{times}}).

(51)

Because
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}
is an orthogonal matrix,
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}
is a spectral decomposition, which leads to

\mathcal{H}_{1}={\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\Big({\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\Big)^{1/2}{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}={\Sigma}_{x}\tilde{U}B_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}.

Similarly, we can show that the matrix
\mathcal{H}_{2}
in (
C-2
) equals
{\Sigma}_{y}\tilde{V}B_{y}^{1/2}\tilde{V}^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}
, where

B_{y}=\text{diag}(\underbrace{1-\Lambda_{1},\ldots,1-\Lambda_{r}}_{r\ \text{times}},\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{q-r\ \text{times}}).

Finally the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{1}={\Sigma}_{x}U\Lambda^{1/2}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}={\Sigma}_{y}V\Lambda^{1/2}
in conjuction with (
C-2
) produces the following representation for
\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}={\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n}\bigg\{U\Lambda^{1/2}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\Lambda^{1/2}V^{T}+U\Lambda^{1/2}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}(\tilde{V}B_{y}\tilde{V}^{T})

\displaystyle\ +(\tilde{U}B_{y}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\Lambda^{1/2}V^{T}

\displaystyle\ +(\tilde{U}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}(\tilde{V}B_{y}\tilde{V}^{T})\bigg\}.

Now recall the sets
E
,
F
, and
G
defined in (
24
) and (
25
) in Section
III-D
, and the decomposition of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}
in (
26
).
From (
26
) it follows that

\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy})=\eta(\mathcal{P}_{E}\{\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}\})+\eta(\mathcal{P}_{F}\{\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}\})+\eta(\mathcal{P}_{G}\{\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}\}).

Recall that for any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, and
S\subset[p]
, we denote by
A_{S^{*}}
the matrix
\mathcal{P}_{S\times[q]}\{A\}
. Then it is not hard to see that

U_{E_{1}^{*}}=U
and
V_{E_{2}^{*}}=V
, which leads to

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{1}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n}\bigg\{U\Lambda^{1/2}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\Lambda^{1/2}V^{T}

\displaystyle\ +U\Lambda^{1/2}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}\Big)

\displaystyle\ +(\tilde{U}_{E_{1}*}B_{y}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\Lambda^{1/2}V^{T}

\displaystyle\ +(\tilde{U}_{E_{1}*}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}\Big)\bigg\}.

(52)

Next, note that
U_{F_{1}*}=0
and
V_{F_{2}*}=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{2}=\frac{1}{n}\bigg\{(\tilde{U}_{F_{1}*}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{F_{2}*})^{T}\Big)\bigg\}.

(53)

Finally, we note that
\mathbf{S}_{3}=(\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{2})
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{1}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n}\bigg\{U\Lambda^{1/2}\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{F_{2}*})^{T}\Big)

\displaystyle\ +(\tilde{U}_{E_{1}*}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{F_{2}*})^{T}\Big)\bigg\}

(54)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{2}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{n}\bigg\{(\tilde{U}_{F_{1}*}B_{y}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}\Lambda^{1/2}V^{T}

\displaystyle\ +(\tilde{U}_{F_{1}*}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}\Big)\bigg\}.

Here the term
\mathbf{S}_{1}
holds the information about
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}=U\Lambda V^{T}
. Its elements are not killed off by co-ordinate thresholding because it contains the Wishart matrix
\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}
which concentrates around
I_{r}
by Bai-Yin law (cf. Theorem 4.7.1 of of
[
42
]
). The only term that contributes to
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,xy}
is
\mathbf{S}_{1}
.
Lemma
7
entails that
\eta(\mathbf{S}_{1})
concentrates around
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
in operator norm. The proof of Lemma
7
is deferred to Appendix
H-B
.

Lemma 7
.

Suppose
s_{x},s_{y}<n
. Then
with probability
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{1})-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{\texttt{Thr}\min\{s_{x},s_{y}\}}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\ +C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{\max\{\sqrt{s_{x}},\sqrt{s_{y}}\}}{\sqrt{n}}.

The entries of
\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
\mathbf{S}_{3}
are linear combinations of the entries of
\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}
, and
\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}
. Since
\mathbf{Z}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
are independent, the entries from the latter matrices are of order
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, and as we will see, they are killed off by the thresholding operator
\eta
. Our main work boils down to showing that thresholding kills off most terms of the noise matrices
\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
\mathbf{S}_{3}
, making
\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{2})\|_{op}
and
\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{3})\|_{op}
small. To that end, we state some general lemmas, which are proved in Appendix
H-B
.
That
\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{2})\|_{op}
and
\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{3})\|_{op}
are small follows as corollaries to this lemmas. Our next lemma provides a sharp concentration bound which is our main tool in analyzing the difficult regime, i.e.,
s_{x}+s_{y}\approx\sqrt{p+q}
case.

Lemma 8
.

Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
are independent standard Gaussian data matrices. Let us also denote
{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}=M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N
where
M\in\mathbb{R}^{p^{\prime}\times p}
and
N\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q^{\prime}}
are fixed matrices so that
p^{\prime}\leq p
and
q^{\prime}\leq q
. Further suppose
\log(p\vee q)=o(n)
and
\log n=o(\sqrt{p}\vee\sqrt{q})
. Let
K_{0}={161}\|M\|^{2}_{op}\|N\|^{2}_{op}
. Suppose
K\geq K_{0}
is such that threshold level
\tau
satisfies
\tau\in[\sqrt{K_{0}},\sqrt{K\log(\max\{p,q\})}/2]
.
Then there exists a constant
C>0
so that with probability
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle\norm{\eta( \mQ;\tau/\sqn)}_{op}\leq C\|M\|_{op}\|N\|_{op}\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{p+q}{n}}\vee\frac{p+q}{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \times e^{-\tau^{2}/K}.

Our next lemma, which also is proved in Appendix
H-B
, handles the easier case when the threshold is exactly of the order
\sqrt{\log(p+q)}
. This thresholding, as we will see, is required
in the easier sparsity regime, i.e.,
s_{x}+s_{y}\ll\sqrt{p+q}
. Although Lemma
9
follows as a corollary to Lemma A.3 of
[
50
]
, we include it here for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
M
,
N
, and
{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}
are as in Lemma
8
, and
\log(p+q)=o(n)
. Further suppose
\|M\|_{op},\|N\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}
where
C>0
is an absolute constant. Let
\tau=\sqrt{C_{1}\log(p+q)}
. Here the tuning parameter
C_{1}>C\mathcal{B}^{4}
where
C>0
is a sufficiently large constant. Then
\eta({\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N};\tau/\sqrt{n})=0
with probability tending to one.

We will need another technical lemma for handling the terms
\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
\mathbf{S}_{3}
.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times p}
and
D=D_{1}\times D_{2}\subset[m]\times[p]
. Then the followings hold:

(a)

\mathcal{P}_{D}(\eta(A))=\eta(\mathcal{P}_{D}(A))
.

(b)

\|\mathcal{P}_{D}(A)\|_{op}\leq\|A\|_{op}

Note that
M=\tilde{U}_{F_{1}*}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}
satisfies

\|M\|_{op}\leq\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1/2}\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}_{F^{*}}\|_{op}\|B_{x}\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\|_{op}.

However,
\|B_{x}\|_{op}\leq 1
. Also because
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
, it follows that
\|{\Sigma}_{x}\|_{op},\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, since
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}
is orthogonal,
\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}\|_{op}=1
.
Part B of Lemma
10
then yields
\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}_{F^{*}}\|_{op}\leq\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}\|_{op}=1
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\|M\|_{op}=\|\tilde{U}_{F_{1}*}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}{\Sigma}_{x}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}.

(55)

Similarly we can show that the matrix
N=\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{F_{2}*})^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}
satisfies
\|N\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
. Because
\mathbf{S}_{2}=\eta(MZ_{1}^{T}Z_{2}N)
by (
53
),
that
\eta(\mathbf{S}_{2})
is small follows immediately from Lemma
8
. Under the conditions of Lemma
8
,
we have

\displaystyle\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{2})\|_{op}\leq

\displaystyle\ C\mathcal{B}^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\dfrac{p+q}{n}}\vee\dfrac{p+q}{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \times e^{-\texttt{Thr}^{2}/K}

(56)

with high probability provided
K\geq 161\mathcal{B}^{4}
and
\texttt{Thr}\in[13\mathcal{B}^{2},\sqrt{K\log(\max\{p,q\})}/2]
. On the other hand, under the setup of Lemma
9
,
P(\|\eta(S_{2})\|_{op}=0)\to_{n}1
.
Lemma
11
, which we prove in Appendix
H-B
, entails that the same holds for
\mathbf{S}_{3}
.

Lemma 11
.

Consider the setup of Lemma
8
. Suppose
K\geq 1288\mathcal{B}^{4}
is such that
\texttt{Thr}\in[36\mathcal{B}^{2},\sqrt{K\log(2\max\{p,q\})}/2]
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
so that with probability tending to one,

\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{3})\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\dfrac{p+q}{n}}\vee\dfrac{p+q}{n}\bigg)e^{-\texttt{Thr}^{2}/K}.

Under the setup of Lemma
9
, on the other hand,
\eta(\|\eta(S_{3})\|_{op})=0
with probability tending to one.

We will now combine all the above lemmas and finish the proof. First, we consider the regime when
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)e
, so that there is thresholding, i.e.,
\texttt{Thr}>0
. We split this regime into two subregimes:
2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}\leq(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)e
and
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq 2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}
.

F-
1
Regime
2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}\leq(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)/e
:

First, we explain why we needed to split the
e(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq p+q
regime into two parts.
Since
s_{x},s_{y}<\sqrt{n}
, Lemma
7
applies. Note that if
\texttt{Thr}\in[36\mathcal{B}^{2},\sqrt{K\log(\max\{p,q\})}/2]
with
K\geq 1288\mathcal{B}^{4}
, then
Lemma
11
and (
F
) also apply.
Therefore it follows that in this case

\displaystyle\|\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy})-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}\bigg(\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})\texttt{Thr}}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{\sqrt{s_{x}+s_{y}}}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{p+q}{n}}\vee\frac{p+q}{n}e^{-\texttt{Thr}^{2}/K}\bigg).

(57)

We will shortly show that under
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)/e
, setting
\texttt{Thr}^{2}=K\log((p+q)/(s_x+s_y)^2)
ensures that the bound in (
F-1
) is small. However, for (
F-1
) to hold,

\texttt{Thr}^{2}
needs to satisfy

\texttt{Thr}^{2}/K\leq\frac{\log(\max\{p,q\}))}{4},

which holds with
\texttt{Thr}^{2}=K\log((p+q)/(s_x+s_y)^2)
if and only if

\log((p+q)/(s_x+s_y)^2)\leq\log(\max\{p,q\}^{1/4}).

Since
\max\{p,q\}\geq(p+q)/2
the above holds when

(p+q)^{3/4}\leq 2^{-1/4}(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}.

Therefore, setting
\texttt{Thr}^{2}=K\log((p+q)/(s_x+s_y)^2)
is useful when we are in the regime
2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}\leq(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)/e
. We will analyze the regime
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}
using separate procedure.

In the
2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}\leq(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)/e
case,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{p+q}{n}}e^{-\texttt{Thr}^{2}/K}=\sqrt{\frac{p+q}{n}}\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}}{(p+q)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{s_{x}+s_{y}}{\sqrt{p+q}}<\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})}{\sqrt{en}}

because
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)/e
, and similarly,

\frac{p+q}{n}e^{-\texttt{Thr}^{2}/K}=\frac{p+q}{n}\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}}{(p+q)}=\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})}{2\sqrt{n}}

since we also assume
s_{x}+s_{y}\leq 2\sqrt{n}
.
The above bounds entail that, in this regime, the first term on the bound in (
F-1
) is the leading term provided
\texttt{Thr}>1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy})-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\mathcal{B}^{2}\bigg(\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})\texttt{Thr}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})\max(\texttt{Thr},1)}{\sqrt{n}}

with probability
1-o(1)
.
Plugging in the value of
Thr
leads to

\displaystyle\|\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy})-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{s_{x}+s_{y}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg(\max\bigg\{K\log(\frac{(s_x+s_y)^2}{p+q}),1\bigg\}\bigg)^{1/2}

in the regime
2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}\leq(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}\leq(p+q)/e
. In our case,
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}/(p+q)\geq e
. Also since
\mathcal{B}>1
by definition of
\mathcal{P}(r,s,p,q,\mathcal{B})
, we also have
K\geq 1
, indicating

\displaystyle\|\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy})-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{s_{x}+s_{y}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg(K\log(\frac{(s_x+s_y)^2}{p+q})\bigg)^{1/2}.

F-
2
Regime
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}<2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}

When
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}<2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}
, of course, the above line of arguments may not work although this indeed is an easier regime because
s_{x}+s_{y}
is less than
\sqrt{(p+q)/\log(p+q)}
. In this regime, we set
\texttt{Thr}=\sqrt{C_{1}\log(p+q)}
where
C_{1}
is a constant depending on
\mathcal{B}
as in Lemma
9
.
For this
\tau
, we have showed that
\|\eta(S_{2})\|_{op}=0
with probability tending to one. Lemma
11
implies the same holds for
\|\eta(S_{3})\|_{op}
as well. Thus from the decomposition of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}
in (
26
), it follows that the asymptotic error occurs only due to the estimation of
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
by
\eta(S_{1})
. Using Lemma
7
, we thus obtain

\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})\max\{\texttt{Thr},1\}}{\sqrt{n}}.

On the other hand, since
(p+q)^{3/4}>2^{-1/4}(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}
, rearranging terms, we have

\displaystyle\log((p+q)/(s_x+s_y)^2)>

\displaystyle\ \Big(\log(p+q)-\log 2\Big)/4

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\ C\log(p+q).

Thus, in the regime
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}<2^{1/4}(p+q)^{3/4}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{s_{x}+s_{y}}{\sqrt{n}}\max\bigg\{\sqrt{C_{1}\log(\frac{p+q}{(s_x+s_y)^2})},1\bigg\}.

(58)

F-
3
Regime
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}>(p+q)/e

It remains to analyze the case when either
(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}>(p+q)/e
. In that case, there is no thresholding, i.e.,
\texttt{Thr}=0
. We will show that the assertions of Theorem
4
holds in this case as well. To that end, note that (
26
) implies

\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq\|S_{1}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}+\|S_{2}+S_{3}\|_{op}.

From the proof of Lemma
7

it follows that
\|S_{1}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}\max\{\sqrt{s_{x}},\sqrt{s_{y}}\}/\sqrt{n}
. For
S_{2}
, we have shown that it is of the form
M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N/n
where
\|M\|_{op},\|N\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
. On the other hand, we showed that
S_{3}=\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{2}
, where the proof of Lemma
11
shows
\mathbf{H}_{1}
and
\mathbf{H}_{2}
are of the form
MAN
where
\|M\|_{op}\|N\|_{op}\leq 2\mathcal{B}^{2}
and
A
is either
[\mathbf{Z}\ \mathbf{Z}_{1}]^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}/n
(for
\mathbf{H}_{1})
or
\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}[\mathbf{Z}\ \mathbf{Z}_{2}]/n
(for
\mathbf{H}_{2}
). Therefore, it is not hard to see that
\|S_{2}+S_{3}\|_{op}
is bounded by

C\mathcal{B}^{2}\Big(\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}[\mathbf{Z}\ \mathbf{Z}_{2}]\|_{op}+\|[\mathbf{Z}\ \mathbf{Z}_{1}]^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{op}\Big).

For standard Gaussian matrices
\mathbf{Z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
it holds that
\|Z_{1}^{T}Z_{2}/n\|_{op}\leq C(\sqrt{(p+q)/n}+(p+q)/n)
with probability
1-o(1)
(cf. Theorem 4.7.1 of of
[
42
]
). Since
r\leq\min\{p,q\}
,
it follows that
\|S_{2}+S_{3}\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}(\sqrt{{p+q}/{n}}+(p+q)/n)
with probability
1-o(1)
. The above discussion leads to

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq

\displaystyle\ C\mathcal{B}^{2}\bigg(\Big(\frac{s_{x}+s_{y}}{n}\Big)^{1/2}+\Big(\frac{p+q}{n}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{p+q}{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 2C\mathcal{B}^{2}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{p+q}{n}\bigg)^{1/2}+\frac{p+q}{n}\bigg)

because
s_{x}+s_{y}<p+q
.
If
(p+q)\leq e(s_{x}+s_{y})^{2}
, the above bound is of the order
(s_{x}+s_{y})/\sqrt{n}
. Thus Theorem
4
follows.

Appendix G
Proof of Corollary
2

Proof of Corollary
2
.

We will first show that there exist
C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}},c_{\mathcal{B}}>0
so that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[r]}\|\widehat{u}_{n,i}-wu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}}\frac{(s_{x}+s_{y})}{\sqrt{n}}\max\bigg\{\bigg(c_{\mathcal{B}}\log(\frac{p+q}{(s_x+s_y)^2})\bigg)^{1/2},1\bigg\}.

(59)

For the sake of simplicity, we denote the matrix
\widehat{U}^{(1)}
in Algorithm
2
by
\widehat{U}
.
Denoting
\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}=\|\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy})-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}
, we note that

\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}){\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U\Lambda V^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}.

Also the matrix
\widehat{U}_{\text{pre}}
defined in Algorithm
2
and
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U
are the matrices corresponding to the
leading
r
singular vectors of
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\eta(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy}){\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}
and
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U\Lambda V^{T}{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}
, respectively.
By Wedin’s sin-theta theorem (we use Theorem 4 of
[
52
]
), for any
1\leq i<r
,

\min_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|\widehat{U}_{i}^{\text{pre}}-w{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}u_{i}\|_{2}\leq\frac{2^{3/2}(2\Lambda_{1}+\epsilon^{\prime}_{n})\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}}{\min\{\Lambda_{i-1}^{2}-\Lambda_{i}^{2},\Lambda_{i}^{2}-\Lambda_{i+1}^{2}\}},

where
\Lambda_{0}
is taken to be
\infty
, and

\min_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|\widehat{U}_{r}^{\text{pre}}-w{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}u_{r}\|_{2}\leq\frac{2^{3/2}(2\Lambda_{1}+\epsilon_{n}^{\prime})\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}}{\Lambda_{r-1}^{2}-\Lambda_{r}^{2}}.

Since
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
,
\min_{i\in[r]}(\Lambda_{i-1}-\Lambda_{i})>\mathcal{B}^{-1}
and
\min_{i\in[r]}\Lambda_{i}>\mathcal{B}^{-1}
. Therefore, for
\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}<1
, we have

\max_{i\in[r]}\min_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|\widehat{U}_{i}^{\text{pre}}-w{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}u_{i}\|_{2}\leq C_{\mathcal{B}}\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}.

We have to show
\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}<1
.
Theorem
4
gives a bound on
\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}
, which can be made smaller than one if the
C_{\mathcal{B}}
in (
23
) is chosen to be sufficiently large. Hence, the above inequality holds.
Because
\widehat{U}_{i}^{\text{pre}}={\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\widehat{u}_{n,i}
, using the fact
\|{\Sigma}_{x}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
, the last display implies

\max_{i\in[r]}\min_{w\in\{\pm 1\}}\|\widehat{u}_{n,i}-wu_{i}\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{B}^{1/2}C_{\mathcal{B}}\epsilon_{n}^{\prime},

which, combined with Theorem
4
, proves (
G
). Now note that the constant
C_{\mathcal{B}}
in (
23
) can be chosen so large such that the right hand side of (
G
) is smaller than
1/(2\mathcal{B}^{2}\sqrt{r})
. Since
\|{\Sigma}_{x}\|_{op}<\mathcal{B}
, it follows that Condition
1
is satisfied, and the rest of the proof then follows from Theorem
1
.
∎

Appendix H
Proof of Auxilliary Lemmas

H-A

Proof of Technical Lemmas for Theorem
2

The following lemma can be verified using elementary linear algebra, and hence its proof is omitted.

Lemma 12
.

Suppose
\Sigma
is of the form (
41
). Then the spectral decomposition of
\Sigma
is as follows:

\Sigma=\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}x_{1}^{(i)}(x_{1}^{(i)})^{T}+\sum_{i=1}^{q-1}x_{2}^{(i)}(x_{2}^{(i)})^{T}+(1+\rho)x_{3}x_{3}^{T}+(1-\rho)x_{4}x_{4}^{T},

where the eigenvectors are of the following form:

1.

For
i\in[p-1]
,
x_{1}^{(i)}=(y_{i},0_{q})
, where
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
forms an orthonormal basis system of the orthogonal space of
\alpha
.

2.

For
i\in[q-1]
,
x_{2}^{(i)}=(0_{p},z_{i})
, where
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{q-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
forms an orthonormal basis system of the orthogonal space of
\beta
.

3.

x_{3}=(\alpha/\sqrt{2},\beta/\sqrt{2})
and
x_{4}=(\alpha/\sqrt{2},-\beta/\sqrt{2})
.

Here for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
0_{k}
denotes the
k
-dimensional vector whose all entries are zero.

Lemma 13
.

Suppose
\Sigma
is as in (
41
). Then
\text{det}(\Sigma)=1-\rho^{2}
and

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}=

\displaystyle\ \begin{bmatrix}I-\alpha\alpha^{T}&0\\
0&I-\beta\beta^{T}\\
\end{bmatrix}+\dfrac{1}{2(1+\rho)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\alpha^{T}&\alpha\beta^{T}\\
\beta\alpha^{T}&\beta\beta^{T}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\ \dfrac{1}{2(1-\rho)}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\alpha^{T}&-\alpha\beta^{T}\\
-\beta\alpha^{T}&\beta\beta^{T}\end{bmatrix}.

Proof of Lemma
13
.

Follows directly from Lemma
12
.
∎

Lemma 14
.

Suppose
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are of the form (
41
) with singular vectors
\alpha_{1}
,
\beta_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
, and
\beta_{2}
, respectively.
Then

Tr(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}^{-1})=p+q+\dfrac{2\rho^{2}}{1-\rho^{2}}\bigg(1-(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})\bigg).

Proof.

Lemma
13
can be used to obtain the form of
\Sigma_{2}^{-1}
, which implies
\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}^{-1}
equals

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}I_{p}&\rho\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}^{T}\\
\rho\beta_{1}\alpha_{1}^{T}&I_{q}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I_{p}+\rho C_{\rho}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{2}^{T}&-C_{\rho}\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}^{T}\\
-C_{\rho}\beta_{2}\alpha_{2}^{T}&I_{q}+\rho C_{\rho}\beta_{2}\beta_{2}^{T},\end{bmatrix}

where

C_{\rho}={\rho}/{1-\rho^{2}}
.
Since
Tr(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma^{-1}_{2})
equals the sum of the two
p\times p
and
q\times q
diagonal submatrices, we obtain that

\displaystyle Tr(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma^{-1}_{2})=

\displaystyle\ Tr\bigg(I_{p}+\rho C_{\rho}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{2}^{T}-\rho C_{\rho}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}^{T}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ +Tr\bigg(I_{q}+\rho C_{\rho}\beta_{2}\beta_{2}^{T}-\rho C_{\rho}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})\beta_{1}\beta_{2}^{T}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ p+q+\dfrac{\rho^{2}}{1-\rho^{2}}\bigg(Tr(\alpha_{2}^{T}\alpha_{2})+Tr(\beta_{2}^{T}\beta_{2})

\displaystyle\ -(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})Tr(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}^{T})-(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})Tr(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}^{T})\bigg),

where we used the linearity of Trace operator, as well as the fact that
Tr(AB)=Tr(BA)
.
Noticing
\|\alpha_{2}\|_{2}=\|\beta_{2}\|_{2}=1
, the result follows.
∎

H-B

Proof of Key Lemmas for Theorem
4

H-B
1
Proof of Lemma
7

Proof of Lemma
7
.

Note that

\displaystyle\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{1})-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{1})-\mathbf{S}_{1}\|_{op}}_{T_{1}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\|\mathbf{S}_{1}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}}_{T_{2}}.

We deal with the term
T_{1}
first.
Recall from (
26
) that
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{E_{1}\times E_{2}}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{xy})
is a sparse matrix. In particular, each row and column of
\mathbf{S}_{1}
can have at most
s_{y}
and
s_{x}
many nonzero elements, respectively.
Now we make use of two elementary facts.
First, for
x\neq 0
,

|\eta(x)-x|\leq{\texttt{Thr}}/{\sqrt{n}}
, and second, for any matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
,

\|A\|_{op}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}|A_{ij}|\wedge\max_{1\leq j\leq q}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|A_{ij}|.

The above results, combined with the row and column sparsity of
\mathbf{S}_{1}
, lead to

\displaystyle T_{1}=

\displaystyle\ \|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{1})-\mathbf{S}_{1}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq p}\|(\mathbf{S}_{1})_{i*}\|_{0}\Big)\wedge\Big(\max_{1\leq j\leq q}\|(\mathbf{S}_{1})_{j}\|_{0}\Big)\frac{\texttt{Thr}}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \min\{s_{x},s_{y}\}\dfrac{\texttt{Thr}}{\sqrt{n}},

which is the first term in the bound of
\|\eta(\mathbf{S}_{1})-{\Sigma}_{xy}\|_{op}
.

Now for
T_{2}
, noting
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}=U\Lambda V^{T}
, observe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{1}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{U\Lambda^{1/2}\left(\frac{\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}}{n}-I_{r}\right)\Lambda^{1/2}V^{T}}_{S_{11}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{U\Lambda^{1/2}\frac{\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}}{n}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}\Big)}_{S_{12}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{(\tilde{U}_{E_{1}*}B_{y}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\frac{\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}}{n}\Lambda^{1/2}V^{T}}_{S_{13}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{(\tilde{U}_{E_{1}*}B_{x}\tilde{U}^{T}){\Sigma}_{x}\frac{\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}}{n}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}\Big)}_{S_{14}}.

It is easy to see that

\displaystyle\|S_{11}\|_{op}\leq

\displaystyle\ \|{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1/2}\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1/2}\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}V\|_{op}\|\Lambda\|_{op}

\displaystyle\ \times\norm{\frac{\mZ^T\mZ}{n}-I_r}_{op}.

Since
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\sim\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
,
{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}
and
{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}
are bounded it operator norm by
\mathcal{B}
. Also,
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}\tilde{U}
and
{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}\tilde{V}
are orthonormal matrices.
Therefore the operator norms of the matrices
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U
,
{\Sigma}_{y}^{1/2}V
, and
\Lambda
are bounded by one.
On the other hand, by Bai-Yin’s law on eigenvalues of Wishart matrices (cf. Theorem 4.7.1 of
[
42
]
),
\|{\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}}/{n}-I_{r}\|_{op}\leq C(\sqrt{r/n}+r/n)
with high probability. Since
r<s_{x}<\sqrt{n}
, clearly
r/n<1
. Thus
\|S_{11}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}C\sqrt{r/n}
with high probability. Hence it suffices to show that the terms
S_{12}
,
S_{13}
, and
S_{14}
are small in operator norm, for which, we will make use of Lemma
4
. First let us consider the case of
S_{12}
. Clearly,

\displaystyle\|S_{12}\|_{op}\leq

\displaystyle\ \|{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1/2}\|_{op}\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U\|_{op}\|\Lambda^{1/2}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\ \times\norm{\frac{\mZ^T\mZ_2}{n}\Sy\slb\tV B_y(\tV_{E_2*})^T\srb}_{op}.

We already mentioned that
\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
, and
\|{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U\|_{op}
and
\|\Lambda\|_{p}
are bounded by one.
Therefore, it follows that

\|S_{12}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}^{1/2}\norm{\frac{\mZ^T\mZ_2}{n}\Sy\slb\tV B_y(\tV_{E_2*})^T\srb}_{op}.

Now we apply Lemma
4
on the term
\mathbf{Z}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}{\Sigma}_{y}\Big(\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}\Big)
with
A=I_{r}
, and
B={\Sigma}_{y}\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}
. Note that
{\Sigma}_{y}
,
\tilde{V}
, and
B_{y}
are full rank matrices, i.e., they have rank
q
. Therefore, the rank of
B
equals rank of

\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*}
. Note that the rows of the matrix
\tilde{V}
are linearly independent because the square matrix
\tilde{V}
has full rank. Therefore, the rank of
\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*}
is
|E_{2}|
, which is
s_{y}
. Hence, the rank of
B
is also
s_{y}
. Also note that
\text{rank}(A)=r\leq s_{y}\leq n
. Therefore Lemma
4
can be applied with
a=r
and
b=s_{y}
. Also,
\|A\|_{op}=1
trivially follows. Using the same arguments which led to (
55
), on the other hand, we can show that
\|B\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
by (
26
).
Therefore Lemma
4
implies that for any
t>0
, the following holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cn)-\exp(-t^2/2)
:

\norm{\frac{\mZ^T\mZ_2}{n}\Sy\slb\tV B_y(\tV_{E_2*})^T\srb}_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}\frac{\max\{\sqrt{s_{y}},t\}}{\sqrt{n}},

which implies
|S_{12}|\leq C\mathcal{B}^{3/2}\max\{\sqrt{s_{y}},t\}/\sqrt{n}
with high probability.
Exchanging the role of
X
and
Y
in the above arguments, we can show that
|S_{13}|\leq C\mathcal{B}^{3/2}\max\{\sqrt{s_{x}},t\}/\sqrt{n}
with high probability. For
S_{14}
, we note that

\|S_{14}\|_{op}\leq\norm{(\tU_{E_1*} B_x\tU^T)\Sx\frac{\mZ_1^T\mZ_2}{n}\Sy\slb\tV B_y(\tV_{E_2*})^T\srb}_{op}.

We intend to apply Lemma
4
with
A={\Sigma}_{x}\tilde{U}B_{x}(\tilde{U}_{E_{1}*})^{T}
and
B={\Sigma}_{y}\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{E_{2}*})^{T}
. Arguing in the lines of the proof for the term
S_{12}
, we can show that
A
and
B
have rank
a=s_{x}
and
b=s_{y}
, respectively. Without loss of generality we assume
s_{y}\geq s_{x}
, which yields
b\geq a
, as requred by Lemma
4
.
Otherwise, we can just take the transpose of
S_{14}
, which leads to
a=s_{y}
and
b=s_{x}
, implying
b\geq a
. Using (
55
), as before, we can show that the operator norms of
A
and
B
are bounded by
\mathcal{B}
. Therefore, Lemma
4
implies that for all
t\geq 0
,

\|S_{14}\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{\max\{\sqrt{s}_{x},\sqrt{s}_{y},t\}}{\sqrt{n}}

with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cn)-\exp(-t^2/2)
. Hence, it follows that with probability
1-o(1)
,

\|\mathbf{S}_{1}-{\Sigma}_{x}^{-1}{\Sigma}_{xy}{\Sigma}_{y}^{-1}\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}\frac{\max\{\sqrt{s_{x}},\sqrt{s_{y}}\}}{\sqrt{n}}.

∎

H-B
2
Proof of Lemma
8

Without loss of generality, we will assume that
p>q
. We will also assume, without loss of generality, that
p^{\prime}=p
and
q^{\prime}=q
. If that is not the case, we can add some zero rows to
M
and zero columns to
N
, respectively, which does not change their operator norm, but ensures
p^{\prime}=p
and
q^{\prime}=q
.
For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
denote the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. We denote an
\epsilon
-net (with respect to Eucledian norm) on any set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
by
T^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{X})
. When
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
, there exists an
\epsilon
-net of
\mathbb{S}^{p-1}
so that

|T^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{S}^{p-1})|\leq\Big(1+2/\epsilon\Big)^{p}.

By
T^{\epsilon}_{p}
, we denote such an
\epsilon
-net. Although
T^{\epsilon}_{p}
may not be unique, that is not necessary for our purpose. For a subset
S\subset[p]
,
T_{p}^{\epsilon}(S)
will denote an
\epsilon
-net of the set

\{x\in\mathbb{S}^{p-1}\ :\ x_{i}=0\text{ if }i\neq 0\}
. Note that each element of the latter set has at most
|S|-1
many degrees of freedom, from which, one can show that

|T^{\epsilon}_{k}(S)|\leq(1+2/\epsilon)^{|S|}
.
The following Fact on
\epsilon
-nets will be very useful for us. The proof is standard and can be found, for example, in
[
42
]
.

Fact 5
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
for
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exist
x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p}
and
y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}
such that

|\langle x,Ay\rangle|\geq(1-2\epsilon)\|A\|_{op}
.

Letting
\mathbf{A}_{n}=\eta(M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N)
,
and using Fact
5
, we obtain that

P\left(\|\mathbf{A}_{n}\|_{op}>\delta\right)\leq P\left(\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x,\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle|\geq(1-2\epsilon)\delta\right)

for any
\delta>0
.
Proceeding like Proposition 15 of
[
1
]
, we fix
1<J_{p,q}\leq\min\{p,q\}
, and introduce
the sets

\displaystyle S_{x}

\displaystyle\ =\{i\in[p]:|x_{i}|\geq\sqrt{J_{p,q}/p}\},

\displaystyle S_{y}

\displaystyle\ =\{i\in[q]:|y_{i}|\geq\sqrt{J_{p,q}/q}\},

(60)

and their complements
S_{x}^{c}=[p]\setminus S_{x}
and
S_{y}^{c}=[q]\setminus S_{y}
. The precise value of
J_{p,q}
will be chosen later.
For any subset
A\subset[k]
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we denote by
x_{A}
the projection of
x
onto
A
, which means
x_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
(x_{A})_{i}=x_{i}
if
i\in A
, and zero otherwise.
Let us denote the projections of
x
and
y
on
S_{x}
,
S_{x}^{c}
,
S_{y}
, and
S_{y}^{c}
, by
x_{S_{x}}
,
x_{S_{x}^{c}}
,
y_{S_{y}}
, and
y_{S_{y}^{c}}
, respectively.
Note that this implies

x=x_{S_{x}}+x_{S_{x}^{c}},\quad y=y_{S_{y}}+y_{S_{y}^{c}},

as well as

x_{S_{x}},x_{S_{x}^{c}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\quad y_{S_{y}},y_{S_{y}^{c}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}.

There are fewer elements the sets
S_{x}
and
S_{y}
compared to their complements. Therefore, we will treat these sets separately.
To that end, we consider the splitting

\displaystyle P\left(\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x,\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle|\geq 4\delta(1-2\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{P\left(\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x_{S_{x}},\mathbf{A}_{n}y_{S_{y}}\rangle|\geq\delta(1-2\epsilon)\right)}_{T_{1}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{P\left(\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x_{S_{x}},\mathbf{A}_{n}y_{S_{y}^{c}}\rangle|\geq\delta(1-2\epsilon)\right)}_{T_{2}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{P\left(\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x_{S_{x}^{c}},\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle|\geq\delta(1-2\epsilon)\right)}_{T_{3}}

(61)

The term
T_{1}
can be bounded by Lemma
15
.

Lemma 15
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are as in
Lemma
8
and

\mathbf{A}_{n}=\eta({\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N})
where

{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}=M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N/n
.
Then for any
\Delta>0
, there exist absolute constants
C,c>0
such that

\displaystyle P\bigg\{\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x_{S_{x}},\mathbf{A}_{n}y_{S_{y}}\rangle|\geq\Delta\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\exp\bigg((p+q)\dfrac{\log(C\da)}{J_{p,q}}

\displaystyle\ -\dfrac{n^{2}\Delta^{2}}{4C\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}(2n+p+q)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ +\dfrac{C}{\Delta^{2}}\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}(n(p+q))^{C}\bigg\{e^{-c(n+q)}+e^{-c(n+p)}\bigg\}

We state another lemma which helps in controlling the terms
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
.

Lemma 16
.

Suppose
M
,
N
,
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
,
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{A}_{n}
are as in Lemma
8
.
Let
K_{0}={161}\|M\|^{2}_{op}\|N\|^{2}_{op}
. Suppose
K>0
is such that
K\geq K_{0}
and moreover,
\tau\in[\sqrt{K_{0}},\sqrt{K\log p}/2]
.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{2}
be either the set
T_{q}^{\epsilon}
or the set
\tilde{T}_{q}^{\epsilon}=\{y_{S_{y}}:y\in T_{q}^{\epsilon}\}
.
Then there exist absolute constants
C,c>0
such that the following holds for any
\Delta>0
:

\displaystyle P\bigg\{\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in\mathcal{T}_{2}}|\langle x_{S^{c}_{x}},\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle|\geq\Delta\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\exp\bigg(C(p+q)-\dfrac{\Delta^{2}n^{2}e^{\tau^{2}/K}}{C\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}J_{p,q}(2n+p+q)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ +\dfrac{C\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}(n(p+q))^{C}\exp\bigg(-c\min(n,\sqrt{p})\bigg).

Note that when
\mathcal{D}=T_{q}^{\epsilon}
, Lemma
16
yields a bound on
T_{3}
. On the other hand, the case
\mathcal{T}_{2}=\tilde{T}_{q}^{\epsilon}
yields a bound on the term

T_{2}^{\prime}=P\left(\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x_{S_{x}^{c}},\mathbf{A}_{n}y_{S_{y}}\rangle|\geq\delta(1-2\epsilon)\right).

(62)

While
T_{2}^{\prime}
is not exactly equal to
T_{2}
, interchanging the role of
x
and
y
in
T_{2}^{\prime}
gives
T_{2}
. Since the upper bound on
T_{2}^{\prime}
given by Lemma
16
is symmetric in
p
and
q
, it is not hard to see that the same bound works for
T_{2}
.

If we let
\epsilon=1/4
, then
\Delta=\delta/2
.
Combining the bounds on
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
, we conclude that the right hand side of (
H-B2
) is
o(1)
if
\Delta^{2}
is larger than some constant multiple of

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{

\displaystyle\frac{(n+p+q)(p+q)}{n^{2}}\Big(\frac{\log J_{p,q}}{J_{p,q}}+J_{p,q}e^{-\tau^{2}/K_{0}}\Big),

\displaystyle\ \frac{(n(p+q))^{C}}{\exp(c\min\{n,\sqrt p\})}\bigg\}\|M\|^{2}_{op}\|N\|_{op}^{2}

where
K_{0}={320}\|M\|^{2}_{op}\|N\|^{2}_{op}
. We will show that the first term dominates the second term.
By our assumption on
\tau
,
\tau^{2}<80\log p\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}
, which implies
\tau^{2}/K_{0}<\log(p\wedge q)/2
, which combined with the fact
J_{p,q}>1
, yields
J_{p,q}\exp(-\tau^2/K_0)>J_{p,q}/\sqrt{p\wedge q}
.
On the other hand, under
p>q
, our assumption on
n
implies
\log n=o(\sqrt{p})
. Also because
p+q=o(\log n)
, it follows that
{(n(p+q))^{C}}{\exp(-c\min\{n,\sqrt p\})}
is small, in particular

\displaystyle\frac{(n+p+q)(p+q)}{n^{2}}\Big(\frac{\log J_{p,q}}{J_{p,q}}+J_{p,q}e^{-\tau^{2}/K_{0}}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \frac{(n+p+q)(p+q)}{n^{2}\sqrt{p\wedge q}}

\displaystyle\gg

\displaystyle\ (n(p+q))^{C}\exp(-c\min\{n,\sqrt p\}).

Therefore, for
P(\|\mathbf{A}_{n}\|_{\delta}>\delta)
to be small,

\displaystyle\delta^{2}>

\displaystyle\ C\min_{1<J_{p,q}<p\wedge q}\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}\frac{(n+p+q)(p+q)}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\Big(\frac{\log J_{p,q}}{J_{p,q}}+J_{p,q}e^{-\tau^{2}/K_{0}}\Big)

suffices. In particular, we choose
J_{p,q}=\exp(\tau^2/(2K_0))
. Note that because
\tau^{2}\leq K_{0}\log(p\wedge q)/2
, this choice of
J_{p,q}
ensures that
J_{p,q}\ll\min\{p,q\}
, as required. The proof follows noting this choice of
J_{p,q}
also implies

\displaystyle\frac{\log J_{p,q}}{J_{p,q}}+J_{p,q}e^{-\tau^{2}/K_{0}}\leq e^{-\tau^{2}/(2.5K_{0})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \bigg\{\exp(\frac{-\tau^2}{40^2\|M\|_{op}^2\|N\|_{op}^2})\bigg\}^{2}.

\hfill\Box

H-B

Proof of Lemma
9

Proof of
9
.

For any
i\in[p]
and
j\in[q]
,

\mathbf{Z}_{1}M_{i*}/\|M_{i*}\|_{2}\sim N(0,I_{n})

and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{j}/\|N_{j}\|_{2}\sim N(0,I_{n})
are independent. In this case, there exist absolute constants
\delta
,
c
and
C>0
, so that (cf. Lemma A.3 of
[
50
]
)

P\Big(\frac{|M_{i*}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{j}|}{\|M_{i*}\|_{2}\|N_{j}\|_{2}}\geq nt\Big)\leq C\exp(-c nt^2)

for all
t\leq\delta
. Since
(M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N)_{ij}=M_{i*}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{j}
, and
\|M_{i*}\|_{2},\|N_{i}\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{B}
, using union bound we obtain

P\Big({|M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N|_{\infty}}\geq nt\Big)\leq C\exp(\log(p'q')-c nt^2/\B^4).

Letting
\tau=\mathcal{B}^{2}\sqrt{C^{\prime}\log(p+q)}
and
t=\tau/\sqrt{n}
, we observe that for our choice of
\tau
,
t<\delta
for all sufficiently large
n
since
\log(p+q)=o(n)
. Therefore, the above inequality leads to

\displaystyle P(\eta({\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N})\neq 0)

\displaystyle\ =P\Big(|{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}|_{\infty}\geq\tau/\sqrt{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\exp(2\log(p'+q')-c C'\log(p+q)).

Because
p^{\prime}\leq p
and
q^{\prime}\leq q
by our assumption on
M
and
N
,
C^{\prime}>2/c
suffices. Hence the proof follows.
∎

H-B
