Proof of Lemma
11

Proof of Lemma
11
.

From the definition of
\mathbf{S}_{3}
in (
26
), and (
F
), it is not hard to see that
\eta(\mathbf{S}_{3})=\eta(\mathbf{H}_{1})+\eta(\mathbf{H}_{2})
.
We will show that
\mathbf{H}_{1}
is of the form
M[\mathbf{Z}\ \mathbf{Z}_{1}]^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N
where
\|M\|_{op}\leq 2\mathcal{B}
and
\|N\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
. Then the first part would follow from Lemma
8
, which, when applied to this case, would imply

\|\eta(\mathbf{H}_{1})\|_{op}\leq C\mathcal{B}^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{\dfrac{p+q}{n}}\vee\dfrac{p+q}{n}\bigg)e^{-\texttt{Thr}^{2}/K}

provided
\texttt{Thr}\in[36\mathcal{B}^{2},\sqrt{K\log(\max{p+r,q})}/2]
and
K\geq 1288\mathcal{B}^{4}
. Since
r<\min\{p,q\}
, the upper bound of
Thr
becomes
\sqrt{K\log(2\max\{p,q\})}/2
.
The proof for
\|\eta(\mathbf{H}_{2})\|_{op}
will follow in a similar way, and hence skipped.

Letting

\displaystyle A_{1}=

\displaystyle\ \Lambda^{1/2}U^{T},\quad A_{2}={\Sigma}_{x}\tilde{U}B_{x}(\tilde{U}_{E_{1}*})^{T},

\displaystyle A_{3}=

\displaystyle\ {\Sigma}_{y}\tilde{V}B_{y}(\tilde{V}_{F_{2}*})^{T},

we note that (
F
) implies
\mathbf{H}_{1}=A_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}^{T}Z_{2}A_{3}+A_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}Z_{2}A_{3}
, which can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{1}=A_{4}^{T}\Big(\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Z}&\mathbf{Z}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\Big)^{T}Z_{2}A_{3},\quad\text{where}\quad A_{4}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

We will now invoke Lemma
8
because

Z_{3}=[\mathbf{Z}\ \mathbf{Z}_{2}]
is a Gaussian data matrix with
n
rows and
p+r\leq 2p
columns, and the matrices
A_{4}
and
A_{3}
are also bounded in operator norm. To see the latter, first, noting
\|A_{4}\|_{op}=\sqrt{\|A_{4}^{T}A_{4}\|_{op}}
, we observe that

\|A_{4}^{T}A_{4}\|_{op}=\|A_{1}^{T}A_{1}+A_{2}^{T}A_{2}\|_{op}\leq\|A_{1}\|_{op}^{2}+\|A_{2}\|_{op}^{2}.

Therefore it suffices to
bound the operator norms of
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
only. Using (
55
), we can show that the operator norm of the matrices of the form
A_{2}
or
A_{3}
is bounded by
\mathcal{B}
for
(X,Y)\sim\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
. Since
{\Sigma}_{x}^{1/2}U
has orthogonal columns, it can be easily seen that
\|A_{1}\|_{op}\leq 1
. Therefore

\|A_{4}\|_{op}\leq\|A_{1}\|_{op}+\|A_{2}\|_{op}\leq 1+\mathcal{B}\leq 2\mathcal{B}

because
\mathcal{B}>1
as per the definition of
\mathcal{P}(r,s_{x},s_{y},\mathcal{B})
.
The proof of the first part now follows by Lemma
8
. Because

\|A_{4}\|_{op}\leq 2\mathcal{B}
and
\|A_{3}\|_{op}\leq\mathcal{B}
, the proof of the second part follows directly from Lemma
9
, and hence skipped.
∎

H-C

Proof of Additional Lemmas for Section
III-C
and Theorem
3

Proof of Lemma
2
.

To prove the current lemma, we will require a result on the concentration of
\alpha
and
\beta
under
\pi_{x}
and
\pi_{y}
. To that end, for
s,m\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
s\leq m
, let us define the set

\mathcal{W}(s,m)=\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\ :\ \|x\|_{0}\in[s/2,2s],\|x\|_{2}\in[0.9,1.1]\bigg\}.

Suppose
\pi_{x}
and
\pi_{y}
are the Rademacher priors on
\alpha
and
\beta
as defined in Section
III-C
. The following lemma then says that
\alpha
and
\beta
concentrates on
\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)
and
\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)
with probability tending to one.

Lemma 17
.

Suppose
s_{x},s_{y}\to\infty
. Then

\lim_{n}\pi_{x}(\alpha\in\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p))=1;\quad\lim_{n}\pi_{y}(\beta\in\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q))=1.

(63)

Here the probability
\pi_{x}(\alpha\in\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p))
depends on
n
through
s_{x}
and
p
. Similarly
\pi_{y}(\beta\in\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q))
depends on
n
through
s_{y}
and
q
.

Recall the definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta}
from (
19
).
Let us consider the class

\mathcal{P}_{sub}(\mathcal{B})=\bigg\{\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta}\ :\ \alpha\in\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p),\ \beta\in\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)\bigg\}.

If
\alpha\in\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)
and
\beta\in\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)
, than
\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}\leq(1.1)^{2}<\mathcal{B}
because
\mathcal{B}>2
.
Therefore (
19
) implies that
(X,Y)\sim\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{sub}(\mathcal{B})
has canonical correlation
\mathcal{B}^{-1}
. Thus
\mathcal{P}_{sub}(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,2s_{x},2s_{y},\mathcal{B})
, implying

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{G}(r,2s_{x},2s_{y},\mathcal{B})^{n}}\mathbb{P}_{n}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \liminf_{n}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{sub}(\mathcal{B})}\mathbb{P}_{n}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1)\Big).

Suppose
\mathcal{F}_{x}
and
\mathcal{F}_{y}
are the Borel
\sigma
-field associated with
\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)
and
\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)
, respectively.
Define the probability measures
\tilde{\pi}_{x}
and
\tilde{\pi}_{y}
on
(\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p),\mathcal{F}_{x})
and
(\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q),\mathcal{F}_{y})
, respectively, by

\tilde{\pi}_{x}(A)=\frac{\pi_{x}(A)}{\pi_{x}(\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p))}\quad\text{for all }A\in\mathcal{F}_{x},

and

\tilde{\pi}_{y}(B)=\frac{\pi_{y}(B)}{\pi_{y}(\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q))}\quad\text{for all }B\in\mathcal{F}_{y}.

Note also that if
\alpha\in\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)
and
\beta\in\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)
, then
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathcal{P}_{sub}(\mathcal{B})
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{sub}(\mathcal{B})}\mathbb{P}_{n}(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \liminf_{n}\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)\\
\times\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1\Big)d\tilde{\pi}_{x}(\alpha)d\tilde{\pi}_{y}(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\liminf_{n}\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)\\
\times\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1\Big)d\pi_{x}(\alpha)d\pi_{y}(\beta)}{\limsup_{n}\Big(\pi_{x}(\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p))\pi_{y}(\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q))\Big)},

whose denominator is one by Lemma
17
.
Denoting
\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)^{c}=\mathbb{R}^{p}\setminus\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)
, we note that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)^{c}}\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1\Big)d\pi_{x}(\alpha)d\pi_{y}(\beta)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 1-\pi_{y}(\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q))\to_{n}0

by Lemma
17
. Similarly, denoting
\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)^{c}=\mathbb{R}^{p}\setminus\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)
, we can show that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)^{c}\times\mathbb{R}^{y}}\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1\Big)d\pi_{x}(\alpha)d\pi_{y}(\beta)\to_{n}0.

Therefore, it holds that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathcal{W}(s_{x},p)\times\mathcal{W}(s_{y},q)}\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=1\Big)d\pi_{x}(\alpha)d\pi_{y}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\&\liminf_{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}\Big(\Phi_{n}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=0\Big)\Big].

Thus the proof follows.

∎

Proof of Lemma
17

Proof of Lemma
17
.

We are going to show (
63
) only for
\pi_{x}
because the proof for
\pi_{y}
follows in the identical manner. Throughout we will denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{x}}
and
\text{var}_{\pi_{x}}
the expectation and variance under
\pi_{x}
.
Note that when
\alpha\sim\pi_{x}
,

\|\alpha\|_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}I[\alpha_{i}\neq 0]
,
where
I[\alpha_{i}\neq 0]
’s are i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables with success probability
s_{x}/p
. Therefore, Chebyshev’s inequality yields that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\pi_{x}\Big(\Big|\left|\left|\alpha\right|\right|_{0}-s_{x}\Big|>s_{x}\epsilon\Big)\leq p\frac{\text{var}_{\pi_{x}}(I[\alpha_{i}\neq 0])}{s_{x}^{2}\epsilon^{2}}=\frac{1-s_{x}/p}{s_{x}\epsilon^{2}},

which goes to zero if
s_{x}\to\infty
.
Therefore, for
\epsilon=1/2
, we have

\pi_{x}\Big(\|\alpha\|_{0}\in[s_{x}/2,2s_{x}]\Big)\leq\pi_{x}\Big(\Big|\left|\left|\alpha\right|\right|_{0}-s_{x}\Big|>s_{x}\epsilon\Big)\to 0.

Also, since
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{x}}[\sum_{i=1}^{p}\alpha_{i}^{2}]=1
, Chebyshev’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\pi_{x}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\alpha_{i}^{2}-1\geq\epsilon\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{\text{var}_{\pi_{x}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\alpha_{i}^{2}\Big)}{\epsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{p.\text{var}_{\pi_{x}}(\alpha_{i}^{2})}{\epsilon^{2}}\leq\frac{p\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{x}}[\alpha_{i}^{4}]}{\epsilon^{2}}=\frac{1}{s_{x}\epsilon^{2}},

which goes to zero if
s_{x}\to\infty
for any fixed
\epsilon>0
. Here (a) uses the fact that
\alpha_{i}
’s are i.i.d. The proof now follows setting
\epsilon=0.1
.

∎

H-C
1
Proof of Lemma
5

Proof of Lemma depends on two auxiliary lemmas. We state and prove these lemmas first.

Lemma 18
.

Suppose
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is a matrix. Let
\mathbb{P}
be the measure induced by the
m
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector and denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}
the corresponding expectation. Then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
we have

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}E_{\mathbb{P}}[H_{j}(AZ)]=e^{t^{T}(A^{2}-I)t/2}.

Proof of Lemma
18
.

The generating function of
H_{w}
has the convergent expansion
[
69
, Proposition 6]

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}H_{j}(x)=\exp\bigg\{t^{T}x-t^{T}t/2\bigg\}

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Therefore,

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}H_{j}(Ax)=\exp\bigg\{t^{T}Ax-t^{T}t/2\bigg\}.

Multiplying both side by the density
d\mathbb{P}
of
\mathbb{P}
and then integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{m}
gives us

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}E_{\mathbb{P}}[H_{j}(AZ)]=E_{\mathbb{P}}\left[e^{t^{T}AZ}\right]e^{-t^{T}t/2}=e^{t^{T}(A^{2}-I)t/2}.

∎

Lemma 19
.

Let
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})
be as defined in (
18
).
Suppose
z=(z_{x},z_{y})
where
z_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}
and
z_{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q}
. Then for any
t\in\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial^{z}_{t}\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2}t^{T}\Big(\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})-I_{p+q}\Big)t\right\}\bigg|_{t=(0,\ldots,0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \begin{cases}\mathcal{B}^{-|z_{x}|}|z_{x}|!\alpha^{z_{x}}\beta^{z_{y}}&\text{if }|z_{x}|=|z_{y}|,\\
0&o.w.\end{cases}

Proof of Lemma
19
.

Let us partition
t
as
(t_{x},t_{y})
where
t_{x}=(t_{x}(1),\ldots,t_{x}(p))\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
t_{y}=(t_{y}(1),\ldots,t_{y}(q))\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
. We then calculate

\frac{t^{T}\Big(\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})-I_{p+q}\Big)t}{2}=\mathcal{B}^{-1}t_{x}^{T}\alpha\beta^{T}t_{y},

which implies

\displaystyle\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2}t^{T}\Big(\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})-I_{p+q}\Big)t\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \exp\Big\{\mathcal{B}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}t_{x}(i)t_{y}(j)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}^{-k}\frac{\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\alpha_{i}t_{x}(i)\Big)^{k}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{q}\beta_{j}t_{y}(j)\Big)^{k}}{k!}

which equals

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{B}^{-k}}{k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
|z_{x}|=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|z_{y}|=k\end{subarray}}\frac{k!}{z_{x}!}\frac{k!}{z_{y}!}\alpha^{z_{x}}\beta^{z_{y}}t_{x}^{z_{x}}t_{y}^{z_{y}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
|z_{x}|=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|z_{y}|=k\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{B}^{-k}}\frac{k!}{z!}\alpha^{z_{x}}\beta^{z_{y}}t_{x}^{z_{x}}t_{y}^{z_{y}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{Z}^{p+q}\\
|z_{x}|=|z_{y}|\end{subarray}}\mathcal{B}^{-|z_{x}|}\frac{|z_{x}|!}{z!}\alpha^{z_{x}}\beta^{z_{y}}t^{z}.

In step (a), we stacked the variables
z_{x}
and
z_{y}
to form
z=(z_{x},z_{y})^{T}
. Note that following the terminologies set in the beginning of Appendix
E
,
z!=z_{x}!z_{y}!
and
t^{z}=t_{x}^{z_{x}}t_{y}^{z_{y}}
. Note that if
|z_{x}|\neq|z_{y}|
, then the term
t^{z}
has zero coefficient in the above expansion.
Thus the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5
.

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},H_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\sim\mathbb{Q}_{n}}\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[H_{w}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}}{d\mathbb{Q}_{n}}\bigg]\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\sim\mathbb{P}_{n,\alpha,\beta}}\Big[H_{w}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}(X_{i},Y_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta},\\
i\in[n]\end{subarray}}\bigg[\prod_{i\in[n]}H_{w_{i}}(X_{i},Y_{i})\bigg]\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}_{(X_{i},Y_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta}}\Big[H_{w_{i}}(X_{i},Y_{i})\Big]\bigg]

where (a) follows because
(X_{i},Y_{i})
’s are independent observations.
Now note that if
\|\alpha\|\|\beta\|_{2}\geq\mathcal{B}
, then (
19
) implies

\mathbb{E}_{(X_{i},Y_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta}}\Big[H_{w_{i}}(X_{i},Y_{i})\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{(X_{i},Y_{i})\sim\mathbb{Q}}\Big[H_{w_{i}}(X_{i},Y_{i})\Big]=0,

where the last step follows because
\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathbb{Q}}[H_{w_{i}}(Z)]=0
for any
i\in[n]
.
If
\|\alpha\|\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}
, then
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})
defined in (
18
) is positive definite, and (
19
) implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(X_{i},Y_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta}}\Big[H_{w_{i}}(X_{i},Y_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{Z}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[H_{w_{i}}\Big(\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})^{1/2}\mathbf{Z}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \ \partial^{w_{i}}_{t}\bigg(\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2}t^{T}\Big(\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})-I_{p+q}\Big)t\right\}\bigg)\bigg|_{t=(0,\ldots,0)}

by Lemma
18
. Here
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})
is as in (
18
), and
\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})
is positive definite because
\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}
, as discussed in Section
III-C
.
Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(X_{i},Y_{i})\sim\mathbb{P}_{\alpha,\beta}}\Big[H_{w_{i}}(X_{i},Y_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 1\{\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}

\displaystyle\ \times\partial^{w_{i}}_{t}\bigg(\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2}t^{T}\Big(\Sigma(\alpha,\beta,1/\mathcal{B})-I_{p+q}\Big)t\right\}\bigg)\bigg|_{t=(0,\ldots,0)}

Lemma
19
gives the form of the partial derivative in the above expression, and implies that the partial derivative is zero unless
|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|
.
Therefore,
\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},H_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}\neq 0
only if
|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|
for all
i\in[n]
. In this case,
|w_{i}|=2|w^{x}_{i}|
is even, and by Lemma
19
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},H_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[1\{\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}\prod_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{B}^{-|w^{x}_{i}|}|w^{x}_{i}|!\alpha^{w^{x}_{i}}\beta^{w^{y}_{i}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \bigg\{\mathcal{B}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w^{x}_{i}|}\prod_{i=1}^{n}|w^{x}_{i}|!\bigg\}

\displaystyle\ \times\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[1\{\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}\alpha^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{x}_{i}}\beta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{y}_{i}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathcal{B}^{-|w|/2}\bigg\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{|w^{x}_{i}|!}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\ \times\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[1\{\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}\alpha^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{x}_{i}}\beta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{y}_{i}}\bigg].

Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},\widehat{H}_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \begin{cases}\frac{\mathcal{B}^{-|w|}}{w!}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[1\{\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}\alpha^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{x}_{i}}\beta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{y}_{i}}\bigg]^{2}\\
\times\bigg\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{|w^{x}_{i}|!}\bigg\}^{2}\quad\text{ if }|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|\text{ for all }i\in[n],\\
\\
0\quad\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}

∎

H-C
2
Proof of Lemma
6

Proof.

Lemma
5
implies that
\mathbb{L}_{n}
belongs to the subspace generated by those
H_{w}
’s whose degree-index
w
has
|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|
for all
i\in[n]
. The degree of the polynomial
H_{w}
is
|w|
, which is even in the above case. Therefore, if
D_{n}\geq 1
is odd,

\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq D_{n}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
equals
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq(D_{n}-1)}\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}
.
Hence, it suffices to compute the norm of
\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq 2\mathcal{D}_{n}}
, where
\mathcal{D}_{n}=\left\lfloor D_{n}/2\right\rfloor
. Suppose
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{n(p+q)}
is such that
|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|
for all
i\in[n]
. Lemma
5
gives

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},\widehat{H}_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\mathcal{B}^{-|w|}}{w!}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\Big[1\{\|\alpha\|_{2}\|\beta\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}\alpha^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{x}_{i}}\beta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{y}_{i}}\Big]\bigg\}^{2}

\displaystyle\ \times\bigg\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{|w^{x}_{i}|!}\bigg\}^{2}.

Consider the pair of replicas
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\pi_{x}
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\pi_{y}
. Letting
W
denote the indicator function of the event

\{\|\alpha_{1}\|_{2}\|\beta_{1}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B},\ \|\alpha_{2}\|_{2}\|\beta_{2}\|_{2}<\mathcal{B}\}
,
we can then write

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{L}_{n},\widehat{H}_{w}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\mathcal{B}^{-|w|}}{w!}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\Big[(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{x}_{i}}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w^{y}_{i}}W\Big]

\displaystyle\ \times\bigg\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{|w^{x}_{i}|!}\bigg\}^{2}.

(64)

Denote by
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Using (
H-C2
), we obtain the following expression:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq 2\mathcal{D}_{n}}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}=\sum_{d=0}^{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathcal{B}^{-2d}\sum_{\overline{d}:\sum d_{i}=d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w:w^{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
w^{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}T_{\overline{d},w}

where

T_{\overline{d},w}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\frac{d_{i}^{2}}{w^{x}_{i}!w^{y}_{i}!}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{w^{x}_{i}}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{w^{y}_{i}}\bigg)\bigg].

Therefore
\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq 2\mathcal{D}_{n}}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{d=0}^{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathcal{B}^{-2d}\sum_{\overline{d}:\sum d_{i}=d}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w:w^{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\\
w^{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q},\\
|w^{x}_{i}|=|w^{y}_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}!}{w^{x}_{i}!}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{w^{x}_{i}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \times\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}!}{w^{y}_{i}!}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{w^{y}_{i}}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{d=0}^{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathcal{B}^{-2d}\sum_{\overline{d}:\sum d_{i}=d}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w^{x}:w^{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}\\
|w^{x}_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}!}{w^{x}_{i}!}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{w^{x}_{i}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ \times\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w^{y}:w^{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q}\\
|w^{y}_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}!}{w^{y}_{i}!}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{w^{y}_{i}}\bigg)\bigg]

In the last step, we used the variables
w^{x}=(w^{x}_{1},\ldots,w^{x}_{n})
, and
w_{y}=(w^{y}_{1},\ldots,w^{y}_{n})
. Suppose
z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}
for each
i\in[n]
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},|z_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}!}{z_{i}!}x^{z_{i}}y^{z_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \prod_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},|z_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{i}!}{z_{i}!}x^{z_{i}}y^{z_{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \prod_{i=1}^{n}(x^{T}y)^{d_{i}}=(x^{T}y)^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}},

where (a) follows from Fact
6
.

Fact 6
.

[Multinomial Theorem]
Suppose
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Then for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\alpha_{i}\Big)^{m}=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},|z|=m}\frac{k!\alpha^{z}}{z!}.

Therefore it follows that

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w^{x}:w^{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}\\
|w^{x}_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}!}{w^{x}_{i}!}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{w^{x}_{i}}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w^{y}:w^{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{q}\\
|w^{y}_{i}|=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{d_{i}!}{w^{y}_{i}!}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{w^{y}_{i}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}},

which implies

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\leq 2\mathcal{D}_{n}}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{d=0}^{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathcal{B}^{-2d}\sum_{\overline{d}:\sum d_{i}=d}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \sum_{d=0}^{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathcal{B}^{-2d}{d+n-1\choose d}\mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})^{d}(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})^{d}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{\pi}\bigg[W\sum_{d=0}^{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\bigg\{{d+n-1\choose d}\bigg(\mathcal{B}^{-2}(\alpha_{1}^{T}\alpha_{2})(\beta_{1}^{T}\beta_{2})\bigg)^{d}\bigg\}\bigg].

where
(a)
follows since the number of
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
|\overline{d}|=d
equals
{n+d-1\choose d}
. Noting
\mathcal{D}_{n}=\left\lfloor D_{n}/2\right\rfloor
, the proof follows.
∎

H-D

Proof of Technical Lemmas for Theorem
4

First, we introduce some additional notations and state some useful results that will be used repeatedly throughout the proof.
Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
. We can write
A
as

A=\begin{bmatrix}A_{*1}&A_{*2}&\cdots&A_{*q}\end{bmatrix}.

We define the vectorization operator as

\text{Vec}(A)=\begin{bmatrix}A_{*1}\\
\cdots\\
A_{*q}\end{bmatrix}.

We will use two well known operations on the vetorization operators, which follow from Section 10.2.2 of
[
70
]
.

Fact 7
.

A.

Trace(A^{T}B)=\text{Vec}(A)^{T}\text{Vec}(B).

B.

\text{Vec}(AXB)=(B^{T}\otimes A)\text{Vec}(X)

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker delta product.

Often times we will also use the fact that
[
71
, Theorem 13.12]

\|A\otimes B\|_{op}=\|A\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}.

(65)

Define the Hadamard product between vectors

x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})
and
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{p})
by

x\circ y=(x_{1}y_{1},\ldots,x_{p}y_{p})^{T}.

Note that Cauchy-Schwarz inequality implies that

\|x\circ y\|_{2}\leq\|x\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}

(66)

We will also often use of Fact
1
, which states
\|AB\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|A\|_{op}^{2}\|B\|_{F}^{2}
.

H-D
1
Proof of Lemma
10

Proof.

The first result is immediate. For the second result, denote by
x_{D}
by the projection of
x
on
R^{D}
. Note that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

\displaystyle\dfrac{x^{T}D(A)y}{\|x\|.\|y\|}=\dfrac{x_{D_{1}}^{T}Ay_{D_{2}}}{\|x\|.\|y\|}\leq\dfrac{x_{D_{1}}^{T}Ay_{D_{2}}}{\|x_{D_{1}}\|.\|y_{D_{2}}\|}

Thus the maximum singular value of
D(A)
is smaller than that of
A
, indicating that

\|D(A)\|\leq\|A\|.

∎

H-D
2
Proof of Lemma
4

First, we state and prove two facts, which are used in the proof of Lemma
4
.

Fact 8
.

Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times s}
are potentially random matrices satisfying
A^{T}A=I_{r}
and
B^{T}B=I_{s}
. Let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
be such that
r,s\leq p
, and
\mathbf{X}\mid A,B
is distributed as a standard Gaussian data matrix. Then the matrix

A^{T}\mathbf{X}B\mid A,B
is distributed as a standard Gaussian data matrix.

Proof of Fact
8
.

\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
is a Gaussian data matrix with covariance
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
if and only if

\text{Vec}(\mathbf{X}^{T})\sim N_{np}(0,I_{n}\otimes\Sigma).

(67)

Now

\text{Vec}((A^{T}\mathbf{X}B)^{T})=\text{Vec}(B^{T}\mathbf{X}^{T}A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}(A^{T}\otimes B^{T})\text{Vec}(\mathbf{X}^{T})

where (a) follows from Fact
7
B. However, since
(A^{T}\otimes B^{T})\in\mathbb{R}^{rs\times np}
, (
67
) implies

(A^{T}\otimes B^{T})\text{Vec}(\mathbf{X}^{T})\mid A,B\sim N_{rs}\Big(0,(A^{T}\otimes B^{T})(A\otimes B)\Big),

but

(A^{T}\otimes B^{T})(A\otimes B)=A^{T}A\otimes B^{T}B=I_{r}\otimes I_{s}=I_{rs}.

Therefore,

\text{Vec}((A^{T}\mathbf{X}B)^{T})\mid A,B\sim N_{rs}(0,I_{rs}).

Then the result follows from (
67
).
∎

In the above fact, it may appear that
A^{T}\mathbf{X}B
is independent of matrices
A
and
B
since its conditional distribution is standard Gaussian. However,
A^{T}\mathbf{X}B
still depends on
A
and
B
through
r
and
s
, which may be random quantities.

Fact 9
.

Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times s}
are such that conditional on
A
and
B
,
\mathbf{X}
is distributed as a standard Gaussian data matrix. Further suppose that the rank of
A
and
B
are
a
and
b
, respectively. Then the following assertion holds:

\frac{\|A^{T}\mathbf{X}B\|_{op}}{\|A\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}}\leq\|\mathbb{Z}\|_{op}

where
\mathbb{Z}\mid A,B
is distributed as a standard Gaussian data matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{a\times b}
.

Proof of Fact
9
.

Suppose
P_{A}
and
P_{B}
are the projection matrices onto the column spaces of
A
and
B
, respectively. Then we can write
P_{A}=V_{A}V_{A}^{T}
and
P_{B}=V_{B}V_{B}^{T}
, where
V_{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times a}
and
V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times b}
are matrices matrices with full column rank so that
V_{A}^{T}V_{A}=I_{a}
and
V_{B}^{T}V_{B}=I_{b}
. Writing
A=P_{A}A
and
B=P_{B}B
, we obtain that

\|A^{T}\mathbf{X}B\|_{op}=\|A^{T}V_{A}V_{A}^{T}\mathbf{X}V_{B}V_{B}^{T}B\|_{op}

which is bounded by

\|A\|_{op}\|V_{A}\|_{op}\|V_{A}^{T}\mathbf{X}V_{B}\|_{op}\|V_{B}\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}.

That
\|V_{A}\|_{op}
and
\|V_{B}\|_{op}
are one follows from the definitions of
V_{A}
and
V_{B}
. Fact
8
implies conditional on
V_{A}
and
V_{B}
,
V_{A}^{T}\mathbf{X}V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{a\times b}
is distributed as a standard Gaussian data matrix. Hence, the proof follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Let us denote the rank of
\mathbf{Z}_{1}D
by
a^{\prime}
. Note that
a^{\prime}\leq\text{rank}(D)=a
. Letting
A=\mathbf{Z}_{1}D
, and applying Fact
9
, we have the bound

\|D^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}B\|_{op}\leq\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}D\|_{op}\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}

where
\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{Z}_{1}
is distributed as a standard Gaussian data matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{a^{\prime}\times b}
. Next we apply Fact
9
again, but now on the term
\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}D\|_{op}
, which leads to

\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}D\|_{op}\leq\|D\|_{op}\|\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\|_{op},

where
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times a}
is a standard Gaussian data matrix. Therefore,

\|D^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}B\|_{op}\leq\|A\|_{op}\|\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\|_{op}\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}.

We use the Gaussian matrix concentration inequality in Fact
2
to show that with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cn)
,

\|\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\|_{op}\leq\sqrt{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{a})
.
Also, for
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{a^{\prime}\times b}
, the first part of Fact
2
implies

\mathbb{P}\Big(\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{op}\leq\sqrt{a^{\prime}}+\sqrt{b}+t\mid\mathbf{Z}_{1}\Big)\geq 1-\exp(-t^2/2)

for any
t>0
.
Since
a^{\prime}\leq a
, and
t
is deterministic, the above implies

\mathbb{P}\Big(\|\mathbf{Z}\|_{op}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+t\Big)\geq 1-\exp(-t^2/2).

Hence, for any
t>0
, we have the following with probability at least
1-\exp(-Cn)-\exp(-t^2/2)
:

\|D^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}B\|_{op}\leq\sqrt{2}\|D\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{a})(\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+t).

Since
a\leq b\leq n
, it follows that

\|D^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}B\|_{op}\leq C\|D\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}\sqrt{n}\max\{\sqrt{b},t\}.

Therefore, the proof follows.
∎

H-D
3
Proof of Lemma
15

Proof of Lemma
15
.

Denoting

\mathcal{T}=\bigg\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\ :\ x^{\prime}=x_{S_{x}},y^{\prime}=y_{S_{y}},x\in T_{p}^{\epsilon},y\in T_{q}^{\epsilon}\bigg\},

we note that

\max_{x\in T^{\epsilon}_{p},y\in T^{\epsilon}_{q}}|\langle x_{S_{x}},\mathbf{A}_{n}y_{S_{y}}\rangle|=|\max_{(x,y)\in T}|\langle x,\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle|.

Therefore it suffices to show that
there exist absolute constants
C,c>0
such that

\displaystyle P\bigg\{\max_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}}|\langle x,\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle|\geq\Delta\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\exp\bigg\{(p+q)\dfrac{\log(C\da)}{J_{p,q}}-\dfrac{n^{2}\Delta^{2}}{4C\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}(2n+p+q)}\bigg\}.

\displaystyle\ +\dfrac{C}{\Delta^{2}}\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}(n(p+q))^{C}\bigg\{e^{-c(n+q)}+e^{-c(n+p)}\bigg\}

Let us denote

\mathcal{Z}_{1}=\text{Vec}(\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T})
,
\mathcal{Z}_{2}=\text{Vec}(\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T})
, and
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{T},\mathcal{Z}^{T}_{2})^{T}
.
Thus

\mathcal{Z}^{T}=\{(\mathcal{Z}_{1})^{T}_{*1},\ldots,(\mathbf{Z}_{1})^{T}_{*p},(\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{T}_{*1},\ldots,(\mathbf{Z}_{1})^{T}_{*q}\}.

Recalling

{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}=\frac{M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N}{n}
, we define

f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})=\Big\langle x,\eta({\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N})y\Big\rangle=\langle x,\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle.

(68)

To obtain a tight concentration inequality for
f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})
, we want to use the following Gaussian concentration lemma due to
[
1
]

Lemma 20

(Corollary 10 of
[
1
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\sim N(0,I_{n})
be a vector of
n
i.i.d. standard Gaussian variables. Suppose
\mathcal{B}
is a finite set and we have functions
F_{b}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
for every
b\in\mathcal{B}
. Assume
\mathcal{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Borel set such that for lebesgue-almost every
(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\mathcal{G}:

\max_{b\in\mathcal{B}}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\gradient F_{b}(\sqrt{t}Z+\sqrt{1-t}Z^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\mathcal{L}.

Then, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
so that for any
\Delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{b\in\mathcal{B}}\absolutevalue{F_b(\Z)-\E F_b(\Z)}\geq\Delta\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C|\mathcal{B}|\exp(-\frac{\Delta^2}{C\mathcal L^2})

\displaystyle\ +\frac{C}{\Delta^{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{b\in\mathcal{B}}(F_{b}(\mathcal{Z})-F_{b}(\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}))^{4}\Big]\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}^{c})^{1/2}.

Here
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
\mathcal{Z}
.

In our case, the index
b
corresponds to
(x,y)
, the set
\mathcal{B}
corresponds to
\mathcal{T}
, and the function
F_{b}(\mathcal{Z})
corresponds to
F_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z})
.
To find the centering and the Lipschitz constant
\mathcal{L}
, we need to compute
\mathbb{E}f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})
and
\triangledown_{\mathcal{Z}}f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})
, respectively.

First, note that since
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
are independent standard Gaussian data matrices,
{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}
. Noting
\mathbb{E}\eta(X)=0
for any symmetric random variable
X
, we deduce

\mathbb{E}\langle x,\mathbf{A}_{n}y\rangle=\langle x,E[\eta({\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N})]y\rangle=0.

Using Lemma
21
we obtain that

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_1}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})\|_{op}\Big\|v\circ\gradient\eta(\vec{(}{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}))\Big\|_{2}

and

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_2}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})\|_{op}\Big\|v\circ\gradient\eta(\vec{(}{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}))\Big\|_{2}

where

v=\vec{(}xy^{T}),\quad g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})=\mathbf{Z}_{2}N\otimes M^{T}/n,

h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})=\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}\otimes N/n.

Because
|\gradient\eta(x)|<1
for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\norm{ v\circ\grad\eta(\Vec(\mQ)) }_{2}\ \leq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{x}\gradient|\eta(x)|\|v\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|v\|_{2}=\|x\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}

since

\|v\|_{2}^{2}=\|xy^{T}\|_{F}^{2}=\|x\|_{2}^{2}\|y\|^{2}_{2}
.
Also, because
\|A\otimes B\|_{op}
equals
\|A\|_{op}\|B\|_{op}
, we have

\displaystyle\|g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})\|^{2}_{op}=\ \frac{\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}N\otimes M^{T}\|_{op}^{2}}{n^{2}}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}N\|_{op}^{2}\|M\|_{op}^{2}}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|^{2}_{op}}{n^{2}}.

(69)

and similarly,

\displaystyle\|h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})\|^{2}_{op}\leq\frac{\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|^{2}_{op}}{n^{2}}.

(70)

Therefore,

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_1}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|x\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}\frac{\|M\|_{op}\|N\|_{op}\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{op}}{n},

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_2}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|x\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}\frac{\|M\|_{op}\|N\|_{op}\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|_{op}}{n}.

Letting
\gradient f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z})
denote
\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z)}{\Z}
, we note that the
above two inequalities imply

\Big\|\gradient f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z})\Big\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|x\|^{2}_{2}\|y\|^{2}_{2}\frac{\|M\|^{2}_{op}\|N\|^{2}_{op}(\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|^{2}_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|^{2}_{op})}{n^{2}}.

Because
\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2}\leq 1
, we have

\Big\|\gradient f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z})\Big\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\|M\|^{2}_{op}\|N\|^{2}_{op}(\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|^{2}_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|^{2}_{op})}{n^{2}}.

(71)

We choose a good set
\mathcal{G}_{1}
where the above bound is small. To that end, we take
\mathcal{G}_{1}
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}=\bigg\{

\displaystyle\ (\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{\prime},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime})\ :\ \tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q},

\displaystyle\ \tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime}\in\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times q},\max\{\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|_{op},\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\|_{op}\}\leq\sqrt{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p}),

\displaystyle\ \max\{\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{op},\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime}\|_{op}\}\leq\sqrt{2}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{q})\bigg\}.

(72)

Let us denote
\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\text{Vec}(\mathbf{Z}_{i}^{T})
and
\tilde{}\mathcal{Z}_{i}=\text{Vec}(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{i}^{T})
.
To apply Lemma
21
, now we define the process

\mathcal{Z}_{i}(t)=\sqrt{t}\tilde{}\mathcal{Z}_{i}+\sqrt{1-t}\tilde{}\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{\prime},\quad t\in[0,1],i=1,2.

Equation
71

implies that on
\mathcal{G}_{1}
,

\norm{\triangledown_\Z f_{x,y}\slb\Z_1(t),\Z_2(t)\srb}_{2}^{2}\leq\dfrac{4\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}(2n+p+q)}{n^{2}}={\mathcal{L}}.

We are now in a position to apply Lemma
21
, which yields that

\displaystyle P\bigg(\max_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}}|f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})|\geq\Delta\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C|\mathcal{T}|\exp\bigg(-\dfrac{\Delta^{2}}{C\mathcal{L}^{2}}\bigg)+\dfrac{C}{\Delta^{2}}E\bigg[\max_{(x,y)\in T}f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{4}\bigg]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ \times P(\mathcal{G}^{c}_{1})^{1/2}.

(73)

From equation 79 of
[
1
]
it follows that
C
can be chosen so large such that

|\mathcal{T}|\leq\exp\bigg((p+q)\dfrac{\log(C\da)}{J_{p,q}}\bigg).

Thus, after plugging in the value of
{\mathcal{L}}
, the first term on the right hand side of (
H-D3
) can be bounded above by

C\exp\bigg\{(p+q)\dfrac{\log(C\da)}{J_{p,q}}-\dfrac{n^{2}\Delta^{2}}{4C\|M\|^{2}\|N\|^{2}(2n+p+q)}\bigg\}.

To bound the second term in (
H-D3
), notice that
Lemma
22
yields the bound

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\max_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}}f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{4}\bigg]\leq C\|M\|_{op}^{4}\|N\|_{op}^{4}(n(p+q))^{C},

whereas Fact
2
leads to the bound

P(\mathcal{G}_{1}^{c})^{1/2}\leq 2\Big(\exp(-c(n+p))+\exp(-c(n+q))\Big).

(74)

Therefore
the proof follows.
∎

Lemma 21
.

Suppose
f_{x,y}
is as defined in (
68
) and

{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}=M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N/n
. Then

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_1}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})\|_{op}\Big\|v\circ\gradient\eta(\vec{(}{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}))\Big\|_{2},

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_2}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})\|_{op}\Big\|v\circ\gradient\eta(\vec{(}{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}))\Big\|_{2}

where

v=\vec{(}xy^{T})
,
g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})=\mathbf{Z}_{2}N\otimes M^{T}/n
, and
h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})=\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}\otimes N/n
.

Proof.

Using
v=\vec{(}xy^{T})
, and the fact that
Tr(AB)=Tr(BA)
, we calculate that

\displaystyle f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})=

\displaystyle\ Tr\bigg(yx^{T}\eta({\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N})\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ Tr\bigg((xy^{T})^{T}\eta\Big(\frac{M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N}{n}\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \text{Vec}(xy^{T})^{T}\text{Vec}\bigg(\eta\Big(\frac{M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N}{n}\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ v^{T}\eta\bigg(\text{Vec}\Big(\frac{M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N}{n}\Big)\bigg).

Fact
7
implies

\vec{(}{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N})=\frac{(N^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T}\otimes M)}{n}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\\
=g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1},

(75)

which yields
f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})=v^{T}\eta(g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1})
.
Noting
v\in\mathbb{R}^{pq}
, we can hence write
f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})
as

f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})=\sum_{i=1}^{pq}v_{i}\eta\bigg([g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})_{i}]^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\bigg).

Let us denote by
\gradient\eta(x)
the derivative of
\eta(x)
evaluated at
x\in\mathbb{R}
. For
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, we denote by
\gradient\eta(A)
the matrix whose
(i,j)
-th entry equals
\gradient\eta(A_{i,j})
.
Then we obtain that for
j\in[np]
,

\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{(\Z_1)_j}=\sum_{i=1}^{pq}v_{i}\gradient\eta\bigg([g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})_{i}]^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\bigg)g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})_{ij},

indicating that

\displaystyle\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_1}=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{pq}v_{i}\gradient\eta\bigg([g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})_{i}]^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\bigg)g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})\bigg[v\circ\gradient\eta\bigg(g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\bigg)\bigg]

where
\circ
implies the Hadamard product. It follows that

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_1}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})\|_{op}\Big\|v\circ\gradient\eta\bigg(g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\bigg)\Big\|_{2}.

Then the first part of the proof follows from (
75
). The proof of the second part follows similarly, and hence, skipped.

Writing
v^{\prime}=Vec(yx^{T})
, we have

\displaystyle f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})=

\displaystyle\ Tr\bigg(\eta\Big(\frac{N^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}}{n}\Big)xy^{T}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ Tr\bigg(xy^{T}\eta\Big(\frac{N^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}}{n}\Big)\bigg),

which equals

\displaystyle Tr\bigg((yx^{T})^{T}\eta\Big(\frac{N^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}}{n}\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \text{Vec}(yx^{T})^{T}\text{Vec}\bigg(\eta\Big(\frac{N^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}}{n}\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (v^{\prime})^{T}\eta\bigg(\text{Vec}\Big(\frac{N^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}}{n}\Big)\bigg).

Fact
7
implies that the above equals

\displaystyle(v^{\prime})^{T}\eta\bigg(\frac{(M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\otimes N^{T})}{n}\mathcal{Z}_{2}\bigg)=

\displaystyle\ (v^{\prime})^{T}\eta\bigg(h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{2}\bigg).

where
h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})=\frac{\mathbf{Z}_{1}M^{T}\otimes N}{n}
.
Thus, similarly we can show that

\displaystyle\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_2}\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \|h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})\|_{op}\Big\|v^{\prime}\circ\gradient\eta\bigg(h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{2}\bigg)\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \|h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})\|_{op}\Big\|\text{Vec}\Big((xy^{T})^{T}\Big)\circ\text{Vec}\bigg(\gradient\eta\bigg(\Big[\frac{M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N}{n}\Big]\bigg)^{T}\bigg)\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \|h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})\|_{op}\Big\|\text{Vec}\Big(xy^{T}\Big)\circ\text{Vec}\bigg(\gradient\eta\bigg(\Big[\frac{M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N}{n}\Big]\bigg)\bigg)\Big\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \|h(\mathbf{Z}_{1})\|_{op}\Big\|v\circ\gradient\eta\bigg(g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{T}\mathcal{Z}_{1}\bigg)\Big\|_{2}.

Therefore, the proof follows.
∎

Lemma 22
.

There exists an absolute constant
C
so that the function
f_{x,y}
defined in (
68
) satisfies

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\max_{\|x\|_{2}\leq 1,\|y\|_{2}\leq 1}f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{4}\bigg]\leq C\|M\|_{op}^{4}\|N\|_{op}^{4}(n(p+q))^{C}.

Proof.

As usual, we let
{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}=M\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N/n
.
Since
\|x\|_{2},\|y\|_{2}\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{4}\leq\norm{\eta(\mQ)}_{op}^{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\|\eta({\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N})\|^{4}_{F}

\displaystyle\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}\|_{F}^{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}n^{-4}\|M\|_{op}^{4}\|N\|_{op}^{4}\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|_{F}^{4}\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{F}^{4}.

Here (a) follows because the operator norm is smaller than the Frobenius norm,
(b)
follows because
|\eta(x)|\leq|x|
, and
(c) follows from Fact
1
.
Since
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
are independent,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\max_{\|x\|_{2}\leq 1,\|y\|_{2}\leq 1}f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{4}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ n^{-4}\|M\|_{op}^{4}\|N\|_{op}^{4}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|_{F}^{4}]\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{F}^{4}].

Now note that since
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
are standard Gaussian data matrices,

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|_{F}^{4}]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[Tr(\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{1})^{2}\Big]\leq k_{1}(n+p)^{k_{2}}

for some absolute constants
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
. We can choose
C
so large such that
k_{1}(n+p)^{k_{2}}\leq C(n+p)^{C}
.
Similarly, we can show that

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{F}^{4}]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[Tr(\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{2}\Big]\leq C(n+q)^{C},

implying

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\max_{\|x\|_{2}\leq 1,\|y\|_{2}\leq 1}f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{4}\bigg]\leq C\|M\|_{op}^{4}\|N\|_{op}^{4}(n(p+q))^{C}

for sufficiently large
C
.
∎

H-D
4
Proof of Lemma
16

Proof.

The framework will be same as the proof of Lemma
15
.
Define
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\times\mathcal{T}_{2}

where

\mathcal{T}_{1}=\bigg\{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\ :\ x^{\prime}=x_{S_{x}},\ x\in T_{p}^{\epsilon}\bigg\}.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
,
\mathcal{Z}
, and
f_{x,y}
be as in Lemma
15
. In this case, the main difference from Lemma
15
is that
|\mathcal{T}|
is much larger. Eventually we will arrive at (
H-D3
) using the concentration inequality in Lemma
21
, but large
|\mathcal{T}|
makes the right hand side of the inequality in (
H-D3
) much larger. Therefore, we require a tighter bound on
\mathcal{L}
, which is the bound on the Lipschitz constant of
\gradient f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z})
on the good set, so that the concentration inequality in (
H-D3
) is still useful. To bound the Lipschitz constant, as before, we bound
\|\triangledown f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})\|^{2}
using
Lemma
21
, which implies that

\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_1}\Big\|_{2}\leq\|g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})\|_{op}\Big\|v\circ\gradient\eta(\vec{(}{\mathbf{Q}}_{M,N}))\Big\|_{2},

where
v=\text{Vec}(xy^{T})
and
g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})=\mathbf{Z}_{2}N\otimes M^{T}/n
.
From (
H-D3
)
it follows that

\|g(\mathbf{Z}_{2})\|_{op}^{2}\leq\frac{\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{op}^{2}}{n^{2}}.

(76)

In Lemma
15
, we bounded
\norm{ v\circ\grad\eta( \Vec(\mQ)) }_{2}
by
\|v\|_{2}
, which was later bounded by
1
.
We require a tighter bound on
\norm{ v\circ\grad\eta( \Vec(\mQ)) }_{2}
this time. Note that
\gradient\eta(z)\leq 1\{|z|\geq\tau/\sqrt{n}\}
at all
z\in\mathbb{R}
for any directional derivative of
\eta
. Noting
\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{J_{p,q}/p}
for
x\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
, we deduce that any
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
and
(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}
satisfy

\displaystyle\norm{ v\circ\grad\eta( \Vec(A)) }_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{j=1}^{q}x_{i}^{2}y_{j}^{2}\eta(A_{i,j})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{J_{p,q}}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{q}y_{j}^{2}\sup_{j\in[q]}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\eta(A_{i,j})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{J_{p,q}}{p}\|y\|_{2}^{2}\sup_{j\in[q]}\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|A_{i,j}|>\tau/\sqrt{n}\},

which is not greater than

{J_{p,q}}\sup_{j\in[q]}\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|A_{i,j}|>\tau/\sqrt{n}\}/p

because
\|y\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1
for
y\in\mathcal{T}_{2}
.

Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_1}\Big\|^{2}_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{2J_{p,q}\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{op}^{2}}{pn^{2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\sup_{j\in[q]}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{\absolutevalue{(\mQ)_{i,j}}>\tau/\sqrt{n}\}\bigg\}.

Similarly, we can show that

\displaystyle\Big\|\partialderivative{f_{x,y}(\Z_1,\Z_2)}{\Z_2}\Big\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{2J_{p,q}\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|_{op}^{2}}{pn^{2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\sup_{j\in[q]}\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{\absolutevalue{(\mQ)_{i,j}}>\tau/\sqrt{n}\}\bigg\}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\Big\|\gradient{f_{x,y}(\Z)}\Big\|^{2}_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{2J_{p,q}\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}(\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}\|_{op}^{2}+\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}\|_{op}^{2})}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle\ \times\sup_{j\in[q]}\frac{\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{\absolutevalue{(\mQ)_{i,j}}>\tau/\sqrt{n}\}\bigg\}}{p}.

We want to define the good set
\mathcal{G}_{2}
of
(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{\prime},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime})
such that

Z_{i}(t)=\sqrt{t}\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{i}+\sqrt{1-t}\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{i}^{\prime},\quad t\in[0,1],i=1,2,

satisfies both

\|\mathbf{Z}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}_{2}(t)\|^{2}_{op}\leq 4(2n+p+q)
and

\sup_{j\in[q]}\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|(M\mathbf{Z}_{1}(t)^{T}\mathbf{Z}_{2}(t)N)_{i,j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}\}\leq 4pe^{-\tau^{2}/K}.

We claim that the above holds if
(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{\prime},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
defined in (
H-D3
), and for all
j\in[q]
,

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|(M\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N)_{i,j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/2\},

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|(M(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{1})^{T}\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{2}N)_{i,j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/2\}\leq 2pe^{-\tau^{2}/K}

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|(M(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{1})^{T}\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N)_{i,j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/2\},

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|(M\tilde{(}\mathbf{Z}_{1})^{T}\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{2}N)_{i,j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/2\}\leq 2pe^{-\tau^{2}/K}.

(77)

The above claim follows from (89) and (90) of
[
1
]
. Therefore we define the good set
\mathcal{G}_{2}
to be the subset of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
where (
H-D4
) is satisfied.
Defining
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(t)=\text{Vec}(\mathbf{Z}_{1}(t)^{T})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{2}(t)=\text{Vec}(\mathbf{Z}_{2}(t)^{T})
, we obtain that for some absolute constant
C>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\gradient f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(t),\mathcal{Z}_{2}(t))\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ qC\underbrace{\dfrac{J_{p,q}(2n+p+q)\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|^{2}_{op}e^{-\tau^{2}/K_{0}}}{n^{2}}}_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}=C\mathcal{L}^{2}

provided
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}
,
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{1}
,
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}
.
Similar to the proof of Lemma
15
, using Lemma
21
, we obtain that there exists an absolute constant
C
so that

\displaystyle P\bigg\{\max_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}}|f_{x,y}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})|\geq\Delta\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C|\mathcal{T}|\exp\bigg(-\dfrac{\Delta^{2}}{C\mathcal{L}^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\ +\dfrac{C}{\Delta^{2}}E\Big[\max_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}}f_{x,y}(\mathbf{Z}_{1},\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{4}\Big]^{1/2}P(\mathcal{G}_{2}^{c})^{1/2}.

(78)

Now since
|\mathcal{T}|\leq|T_{p}^{\epsilon}|\times|T_{q}^{\epsilon}|
, and for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
\epsilon
-net
T_{k}^{\epsilon}
is chosen so as to satisfy

|T_{k}^{\epsilon}|\leq(1+2/\epsilon)^{k}
, we have

|\mathcal{T}|\leq(1+2/\epsilon)^{p+q}
. Therefore, we conclude that the first term of the bound in (
H-D4
) is not larger than

C\exp\bigg(-\dfrac{\Delta^{2}}{C\mathcal{L}^{2}}+(p+q)\log(1+2/\e)\bigg).

Rest of the proof is devoted to bounding the second term of the bound in (
H-D4
). The expectation term can be bounded easily using
Lemma
22
, which yields

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\max_{(x,y)\in T}f_{x,y}(\mathbf{Z}_{1},\mathbf{Z}_{2})^{4}\bigg]\leq C\|M\|_{op}^{4}\|N\|^{4}_{op}\{n(p+q)\}^{C}.

We will now show that
P(\mathcal{G}_{2}^{c})

is small.
Note that by definition,
\mathcal{G}_{2}=\mathcal{G}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}
, where
\mathcal{V}
is the set of
(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{\prime},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime})
, which satisfies the equation system (
H-D4
). Notice that by (
74
), we already have
P(\mathcal{G}_{1}^{c})\leq e^{-c(n+p)}+e^{-c(n+q)}
for some
c>0
. Thus it suffices to show that
P(\mathcal{V}^{c})
is small.
To this end, note that since
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}_{1},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2},\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}^{\prime}
are independent, (
H-D4
) implies

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{V}^{c})\leq 4P\bigg(

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\Big\{|M^{T}_{i*}\tilde{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/4\Big\}>2pe^{-\tau^{2}/K}

\displaystyle\ \text{ for all }j\in[q]\bigg).

Defining the set

\mathcal{A}_{j}=\bigg\{\|\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{*j}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{n}\|N_{*j}\|_{2}\bigg\}
, we bound the above probability as follows:

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{V}^{c})\leq 4\sum_{j=1}^{q}P\bigg(

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|M^{T}_{i*}\tilde{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/4\}

\displaystyle>2p\exp(-\tau^2/K_0)\ \bigg|\ \tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}\bigg)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\ 4\sum_{j=1}^{q}P\Big(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}^{c}\Big).

(79)

Now note that
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{*j}\sim N_{n}\Big(0,\|N_{*j}\|_{2}^{2}I_{n}\Big)
, or

{\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{*j}}/{\|N_{*j}\|_{2}}\sim N_{n}(0,I_{n})
.
Therefore, there exists a universal constant
c>0
so that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{q}P(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}^{c})=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j=1}^{q}P\bigg(\|N_{*j}\|_{2}^{-1}\|\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{*j}\|_{2}>2\sqrt{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ q\exp(-cn),

(80)

where the last bound is due to the Chi-square tail bound in Fact
4
(see also Lemma 1 of
[
72
]
and Lemma 12 of
[
1
]
).
Therefore, it only remains to bound the first term in (
H-D4
).
We begin with an expansion of
|M^{T}_{i*}\tilde{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{j}|
as follows

\displaystyle|M^{T}_{i*}\tilde{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{j}|=

\displaystyle\ \bigg|\sum_{l=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{il}(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1})_{kl}(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N)_{kj}\bigg|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \bigg|\sum_{l=1}^{p}M_{il}\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1})_{kl}(\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N)_{kj}}_{\Psi^{j}_{l}}\bigg|.

Since
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}
and
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}
are independent,
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{1}
conditioned on
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}
is still a standard Gaussian data matrix.
Hence, for
l\in[p]
, conditional on
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}
,
\Psi_{l}^{j}
’s are independent
N(0,\|\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{*j}\|^{2}_{2})
random variables. As a result, for each
l\in[p]
and
j\in[q]
,
\Psi_{l}^{j}
can be written as
\|\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{*j}\|_{2}\mathbb{Z}_{l}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{l}=\Psi_{l}^{j}/\|\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}N_{*j}\|_{2}
, and
\mathbb{\mathbf{Z}}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p}\mid\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}N(0,1)
.
Noting
\|N_{j}\|_{2}\leq\|N\|_{op}
for every
j\in[q]
, we derive the following bound provided
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|M^{T}_{i*}\tilde{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/4\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}1\Big[\|\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{*j}\|_{2}\Big|\sum_{l=1}^{p}M_{il}\mathbb{Z}_{l}\Big|>\tau\sqrt{n}/4\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{p}1\Big[\sqrt{2}\|N\|_{op}\Big|\sum_{l=1}^{p}M_{il}\mathbb{Z}_{l}\Big|>\tau/4\Big].

Defining

f(x)\equiv f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{1\Big[|\sum_{l=1}^{p}M_{il}x_{l}|>\tau/(4\sqrt{2}\|N\|_{op})\Big]}{p},

(81)

we notice that the above calculations implies conditional on
\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
,

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{p}|M^{T}_{i*}\tilde{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/4]}{p}\leq f(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle P\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{p}1\{|M^{T}_{i*}\tilde{Z}_{1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{2}N_{j}|>\tau\sqrt{n}/4\}>2pe^{-\tau^{2}/K}\mid\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ P\Big(f(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p})>2\exp(-\tau^2/K)\mid\tilde{}\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}\Big),

(82)

which is is bounded by
\exp(-2\sqrt{p})

by Lemma
23
. Therefore, (
H-D4
), (
H-D4
), and (
H-D4
) jointly imply that

P(\mathcal{V}^{c})\leq 4q\exp(-cn)+4q\exp(-2\sqrt{p}).

Therefore
P(\mathcal{G}_{2}^{c})
is bounded by

\displaystyle\exp(-c(n+p))+\exp(-c(n+q))+4q\exp(-cn)

\displaystyle\ +4q\exp(-2\sqrt{p})\leq 4q\exp(-c\min\{n,\sqrt{p}\}),

which completes the proof.

∎

Lemma 23
.

Suppose
160\|M\|^{2}_{op}\|N\|^{2}_{op}<K,\tau^{2}
and
\tau<\sqrt{K\log p}/2
. Further suppose
\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots\mathbb{Z}_{p}
are independent standard Gaussian random variables. Then the function
f
defined in (
81
) satisfies

\mathbb{P}\Big(f(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p})>2\exp(-\tau^2/K)\Big)\leq\exp(-2\sqrt{p}).

Proof of Lemma
23
.

Note that
pf(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is a sum of dependent Bernoulli random variables. Therefore the traditional Chernoff’s or Hoeffding’s bound for inependent Bernoulli random variables will not apply.
We use a generalized version of Chernoff’s inequality, originally due to
[
65
]
(also discussed by
[
73
,
74
]
among others), which applies to weakly dependent Bernoulli random variables.

Lemma 24

(
[
65
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
be Bernoulli random variables and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Suppose there exists
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
such that for any
\mathcal{B}\subset[p]
, the following assertion holds:

\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i}\Big]\leq\delta^{|\mathcal{B}|}.

(83)

For
x,y\in(0,1)
, we denote

D(x\mid\mid y)=y\log(y/x)+(1-y)\log((1-y)/(1-x)).

Then we have

\mathbb{P}\bigg[\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{p}X_{i}}{p}\geq\epsilon\bigg]\leq\exp\Big(-pD(\delta\mid\mid\epsilon)\Big).

Note that if we take
X_{i}=1\{|\sum_{l=1}^{p}M_{il}\mathbb{Z}_{l}|>\tau/(4\sqrt{2}\|N\|_{op})\}
and
\epsilon=2\exp(-\tau^2/K)
, then the above lemma can be applied to bound
P(f(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p})>2\exp(-\tau^2/K))
provided (
83
) holds, which will be referred as the weak dependence Condition from now on. Suppose
|\mathcal{B}|=k
. For the sake of simplicity, we take
\mathcal{B}=\{1,\ldots,k\}
. The arguments, which are to follow, would hold for any other choice of
\mathcal{B}
as well as long as
\|\mathcal{B}|=k
. Denote by
M_{k}
the submatrix of
M
containing only the first
k
rows of
M
. Let us denote
\mathbb{Z}_{1:k}=(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{k})
. Letting
t=\tau/(4\sqrt{2}\|N\|_{op})
, we observe that for our choice of
X_{i}
’s,
\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i}]
equals

\displaystyle P\Big(|M_{i*}^{T}\mathbb{Z}_{1:k}|>t,\ l\in[k]\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\ P\Big(\mathbb{Z}_{1:k}^{T}M_{k}^{T}M_{k}\mathbb{Z}_{1:k}>kt^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ P\Big(\|M_{k}^{T}M_{k}\|_{op}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{\mathbf{Z}}^{2}_{l}>kt^{2}\Big).

The operator norm
\|M_{k}^{T}M_{k}\|_{op}
equals
\|M_{k}\|_{op}^{2}
, which is bounded by
\|M\|_{op}^{2}
by Lemma
10
B. Therefore, the right hand side of the last display is bounded by
P(\sum_{l=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}_{l}^{2}>kt^{2}/\|M\|_{op}^{2})
.
By Chi-square tail bounds (see for instance Fact
4
), the latter probability is bounded above by
\exp(-kt^2/(5\|M\|_{op}^2))
for all
t>\sqrt{5}\|M\|_{op}
. Since
t=\tau/(4\sqrt{2}\|N\|_{op})
, note that
\tau>\sqrt{160}\|M\|_{op}\|N\|_{op}
suffices. For such
\tau
, we have thus shown that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i}\Big]\leq\exp(-|\mathcal B|\frac{\tau^2}{160\|M\|_{op}^2\|N\|_{op}^2}).

Thus our

\delta=\exp(-\frac{\tau^2}{160\|M\|_{op}^2\|N\|_{op}^2})
,
which is less than
\epsilon/2=\exp(-\tau^2/K)
because
K>160\|M\|_{op}^{2}\|N\|_{op}^{2}
. Thus our
(\delta,\epsilon)
pair satisfies the weak dependence condition. Therefore by
Lemma
24
, it follows that

\mathbb{P}\Big(f(\mathbb{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{p})>2\exp(-\tau^2/K)\Big)\leq\exp(-p D(\delta\mm\epsilon)).

We will now use the lower bound
D(x\mid\mid y)\geq 2(x-y)^{2}
for
x,y\in(0,1)
. Because
|\delta-\epsilon|\leq\epsilon/2
,
D(\delta\mid\mid\epsilon)\geq\epsilon^{2}/2
, indicating

pD(\delta\mid\mid\epsilon)\geq 2p\exp(-{2\tau^2}/{K}),

which is greater than
2\sqrt{p}
if
2\tau^{2}/K\leq\log p/2
, or equivalently
\tau^{2}\leq(K\log p)/4
.
Therefore, the current lemma follows.
∎

Nilanjana Laha

received a Bachelor of Statistics in 2012 and a Master of Statistics in 2014 from the Indian Statistical Institute, Kolkata. Then she received a Ph.D. in statistics in 2019 from the University of Washington, Seattle. She was a postdoctoral research fellow at the department of Biostatistics at Harvard university from 2019 to 2022. She is currently an assistant professor in Statistics at Texas A & M University. Her
research interests include dynamic treatment regimes, high dimensional association, and shape constrained inference.

Rajarshi Mukherjee

received a Bachelor of Statistics in 2007 and a Master of Statistics in 2009 from the Indian Statistical Institute, Kolkata. He received his Ph.D. degree in Bisostatistics from Harvard University in 2014. He was a Stein fellow in the department of Statistics at Stanford University from 2014 to 2017. He was an assistant professor at the division of Biostatistics at the University of California, Berkeley, from 2017 to 2018. Since 2018, he has been an assistant professor at the department of Biostatistics at Harvard University. His research interests primarily lie in structured signal detection problems in high dimensional and network models, and functional estimation and adaptation theory in nonparametric statistics.
